Housing Market Analysis 
MA-05 Overview 
Housing Market Analysis Overview 
As part of the Consolidated Plan (Con Plan) process, grantees conduct a comprehensive assessment of 
the jurisdiction’s housing, economic, and demographic conditions within the Needs Assessment and 
Market Analysis sections. Grantees are required to gather and analyze extensive data to prepare their 
Consolidated Plans. The information compiled in the Needs Assessment and Market Analysis serves as 
the foundation for the grantee’s Strategic Plan, as well as for policy and investment decisions. 
The Con Plan is developed for HUD using the HUD eCon Plan Template. Community Planning and 
Development (CPD) Maps provide grantees with population, housing, and economic data collected from 
the U.S. Census Bureau, HUD, and other federal agencies. Data for the Needs Assessment and Market 
Analysis sections are drawn from multiple sources, including information provided by agencies, City 
departments or divisions, and local nonprofits. 
Data Sources Used 
• 
eCon Planning Suite Data Sources 
o 
American Community Survey (ACS): Conducted annually by the U.S. Census Bureau, the 
ACS collects data from a sample of the population and provides a wide range of 
statistics on household demographics, economics, and housing conditions. Data is 
available at various geographic levels, down to the census block group. 
o 
Comprehensive Housing Affordability Strategy (CHAS): CHAS data is a special tabulation 
of ACS data for HUD, illustrating the extent of housing problems and needs, particularly 
for low-income households. 
o 
Homeless Data Exchange/Annual Homeless Assessment Report (AHAR): HUD 
collaborates with communities to collect data on homeless populations through the 
Point-In-Time (PIT) and Housing Inventory Count (HIC). The AHAR publishes annual 
figures at the national, state, and Continuum of Care (CoC) levels, including numbers of 
homeless persons, chronically homeless individuals, veterans, and homeless children 
and youth. 
o 
Fair Market Rents (FMR): FMRs are gross rent estimates published annually by HUD and 
used by Public Housing Authorities to set payment standards for the Housing Choice 
Voucher program. FMRs also determine rent ceilings for the HOME rental assistance 
program and other HUD initiatives. HUD calculates FMRs using data from the Decennial 
Census, the ACS, and random digit dialing telephone surveys. 

 
  Consolidated Plan 
CHEYENNE 
    63 
OMB Control No: 2506-0117 (exp. 09/30/2021) 
• 
HOME Program Rent and Income Limits: These limits are used to determine eligibility and 
affordability for HUD programs. 
The Housing & Community Development (H&CD) staff utilized available data to predict trends and 
supplemented this analysis with findings from the City’s Analysis of Impediments to Fair Housing Study 
(AI), updated this year by the University of Wyoming Center for Business and Economic Analysis (CEBA). 
CEBA conducted an in-depth analysis of local and state trends, many of which are reflected in the 
Market Analysis section of the Con Plan. The updated AI is available on the City’s H&CD webpage under 
the “Learn More About: Analysis of Impediments to Fair Housing” tab. 
When additional data from other sections is used, the alternative source will be identified accordingly.

MA-10 Number of Housing Units – 91.210(a)&(b)(2) 
Introduction 
Cheyenne’s housing stock includes about 30,000 residential units, primarily single-family detached 
homes, which make up roughly two-thirds of all units and reflect the city’s historic preference for low-
density neighborhoods. Single-family-attached homes are limited, and apartment buildings with five or 
more units account for only about 10% of the total, underscoring a lack of diverse housing options. 
Much of the housing stock is aging, with nearly half of all units built before 1980. This highlights a need 
for rehabilitation and contributes to a shortage of quality, available homes. The shortage is especially 
acute for low- and moderate-income households, who face limited access not just due to overall supply, 
but because affordable units are particularly scarce. 
 
As of 2025, Cheyenne offers 542 subsidized rental units and 533 units that accept Section 8 vouchers, but 
the demand far exceeds supply, resulting in long waitlists and ongoing challenges for families, seniors, 
and people with disabilities. This situation emphasizes the need for more and varied affordable housing 
throughout the city.

Describe the number and targeting (income level/type of family served) of units 
assisted with federal, state, and local programs. 
Cheyenne, Wyoming utilizes a mix of federal, state, and local programs to assist a wide range of low- 
and moderate-income residents with affordable housing. The number and targeting of Federal, State, 
and Local Assisted Housing Units are as follows: 
Subsidized Rental Units 
• 
There are 8 subsidized properties in Cheyenne, totaling 542 units. These are primarily targeted 
to low-income households, with some properties specifically serving seniors and persons with 
disabilities. The largest concentration is in zip code 82001 (Central Cheyenne), which includes 
448 units, mostly managed by the Cheyenne Housing Authority. 
• 
Subsidized properties are distributed as follows: 
o 82001 (Central): 4 buildings, 448 units 
o 82007 (South): 1 building, 4 units 
o 82009 (North): 3 buildings, 90 units (including senior/disabled housing) 
Voucher-Accepting Units 
• 
Cheyenne has 15 properties that accept Section 8 Housing Choice Vouchers, with a total of 533 
units across three main zip codes. These serve income-qualified families, including seniors and 
people with disabilities. Below is a breakdown of the number of buildings and units by zip code: 
o 82001: 6 buildings, 119 units 
o 82007: 7 buildings, 299 units 
o 82009: 2 buildings, 113 units 

 
  Consolidated Plan 
CHEYENNE 
    67 
OMB Control No: 2506-0117 (exp. 09/30/2021) 
Overall Affordable Housing 
According to Affordable Housing Online, Cheyenne has 1,688 affordable units, including both subsidized 
and voucher-accepting units, covering a broad spectrum of low-income residents, including seniors with 
disabilities, and low-income individuals and families. Some properties and programs are specifically 
designed for seniors or persons with disabilities, such as Heritage Court and Indian Hills Manor, which is 
run by the Cheyenne Housing Authority. Most of these units have waiting lists, although specific data 
about occupancy was not available online. 
Provide an assessment of units expected to be lost from the affordable housing 
inventory for any reason, such as expiration of Section 8 contracts. 
According to the Director of the Cheyenne Housing Authority (CHA), there are currently no affordable 
housing units in Cheyenne expected to be lost due to the expiration of Section 8 contracts or similar 
agreements. While four Section 8 contracts are set to expire in Wyoming, none of these properties are 
located in Cheyenne. This means the city’s affordable housing inventory is not at immediate risk from 
expiring federal rental assistance contracts. Cheyenne’s affordable housing stock remains stable with 
respect to expiring federal contracts; however, the broader statewide increase in demand and potential 
losses elsewhere underscores the need for ongoing vigilance and coordinated housing policy efforts. 
Does the availability of housing units meet the needs of the population? 
Mobile and manufactured homes represent a small segment, accounting for just 2% of all housing units. 
While these homes provide a lower-cost alternative, typically priced between $25,000 and $80,000, 
their future is challenged by rising rents, restrictive zoning, and utility challenges which hinder the 
development of new parks and the preservation of affordability. Policy changes could help protect and 
expand this housing option. 
Recent development has increased the city’s total housing units from 30,010 to 32,201, according to the 
2023 ACS 1-year estimates, driven by new construction of both apartments and single-family homes. 
However, much of this new supply is market-rate and remains out of reach for many low- and 
moderate-income (LMI) residents. As of April 2025, Cheyenne’s median home value is $372,516, with a 
median listing price of $422,000, further underscoring affordability challenges for LMI households. 
Rental housing also faces affordability constraints. Of Cheyenne’s 9,655 rental units, only 8.3% are 
affordable to households earning 30% of the area median income. Most affordable rentals are 
accessible only to those earning 50–80% of the HUD Area Median Family Income (HAMFI), leaving very 
few options for the lowest-income renters. New apartment complexes are under construction, with 
rents ranging from $600 to $1,637, but these rates remain high for those at LMI levels. There is a 
growing need for smaller units and accessible, maintenance-free living-especially for singles, seniors, 
and small families. 

 
  Consolidated Plan 
CHEYENNE 
    68 
OMB Control No: 2506-0117 (exp. 09/30/2021) 
Describe the need for specific types of housing: 
Cheyenne’s current supply of affordable housing units does not meet the needs of its population, 
especially for low-income families, seniors, and persons with disabilities. The City has 542 subsidized 
rental units and 533 units that accept Section 8 vouchers, but demand far exceeds supply, as shown by 
long waitlists and inconsistent reporting on unit availability. The total of 1,688 affordable units, including 
both subsidized and voucher-accepting properties, remains insufficient given the growing population 
and rising rates of homelessness. This shortage is most pronounced in central and south Cheyenne.  
A major barrier to expanding affordable housing is the limited availability of land zoned for such 
developments, as well as regulatory restrictions. Rental units for Extremely Low Income (ELI) renters are 
especially needed. According to NLIHC’s 2024 Wyoming Housing Profile, there are 18,176 ELI renter 
households in Wyoming, but only 13.5% of affordable homes are available to them. Wyoming is 
experiencing a shortage of 8,866 affordable rental homes for the state’s ELI renter population. In 
Laramie County, about 13% of residents are disabled, and seniors 65 and over make up around 16% of 
the population—both groups that are particularly vulnerable to housing instability. Despite the 
placement of 521 multifamily affordable housing units between 2019 and 2023 using federal funds, the 
gap remains significant. 
A key challenge is ensuring that new developments serve the most vulnerable populations. Cheyenne 
should encourage developers to include accessible units and prioritize projects that directly benefit ELI 
renters, seniors, and persons with disabilities. This can be supported by providing incentives and 
targeting funding to developments that meet these needs. Actively leveraging federal and state 
resources, such as Low-Income Housing Tax Credits (LIHTC) is key essential to expanding the affordable 
housing stock. 
Alternative housing options, such as mobile and manufactured homes, townhomes, and new 
apartments, provide solutions to reducing the City’s housing shortage but are limited by high prices, 
zoning restrictions, and escalating rents. For example, while mobile homes represent one of the most 
affordable pathways to homeownership in Cheyenne, there is currently no zoning by right for mobile 
home parks in either Laramie County or the City of Cheyenne. This regulatory barrier restricts the 
expansion of this critical entry-level housing style. Effective planning and rezoning agricultural land on 
the edge of the city could make mobile home park development feasible, expanding the housing stock 
for low-income residents while minimizing community resistance. 
Similarly, higher-density options like townhomes and cottages could help address the shortage, but 
current prices remain out of reach for many residents due to high construction costs and zoning 
limitations. New apartment developments are underway, but with rents ranging from $600 to $1,637, 
they are often unaffordable for many in need. MA-15 Cost of Housing – 91.310(a)

Introduction: 
Cheyenne’s housing market continues to suffer from rising costs, with recent data showing significant 
affordability challenges for both homebuyers and renters. According to the 2023 ACS 1-Year Estimates, 
the city’s median home value was $340,800, while the ACS 5-Year Profile reported a median of 
$311,200. More recent figures from Zillow show the median home value reached $372,516 in April 
2025, a 3.4% increase over the past year. Realtor.com lists the median listing price even higher at 
$425,000, with a median price per square foot of $1921. The median sold price stands at $351,817, 
underscoring the persistent upward pressure on home prices. 
This escalation in home values is largely driven by a shortage of affordable housing, making 
homeownership increasingly unattainable for many Cheyenne residents. Rental affordability is also a 
growing concern: nearly half of Laramie County renters are cost-burdened, with median rents rising 42% 
from 2010 to 2021, disproportionately impacting low- and moderate-income (LMI) households. The 
demand for subsidized and Section 8 rental units far exceeds supply, as reflected in long waitlists.

Is there sufficient housing for households at all income levels? 
Cheyenne does not have enough housing for all income levels. Home prices and rents have risen sharply, 
with the median home value at $372,516 in April 2025 and average rents up 42% since 2010. Nearly half 
of renters are cost-burdened, spending more than 30% of their income on housing. Affordable rental 
units, especially for low- and moderate-income households, are in short supply, and demand for 
subsidized housing far exceeds availability. Federal rent limits and subsidies are not keeping pace with 

 
  Consolidated Plan 
CHEYENNE 
    71 
OMB Control No: 2506-0117 (exp. 09/30/2021) 
local market rents, making it difficult for lower-income residents to find affordable options. Overall, 
Cheyenne faces a significant shortage of affordable housing, particularly for those with the greatest 
need, and targeted strategies are required to address these gaps. 
How is the affordability of housing likely to change considering changes to home 
values and/or rents? 
Housing affordability in Cheyenne is becoming increasingly challenging as both home values and rents 
continue to rise. As of April 2025, the median home value in Cheyenne is $372,516, reflecting a 3.4% 
increase over the past year1. This growth follows a trend from 2023, when median values ranged 
between $311,200 and $340,800, indicating a steady upward trajectory. Similarly, rental costs have seen 
significant increases: the average rent in Cheyenne is now $1,500 per month, up by $405 compared to 
the previous year. 
These rising costs mean that more residents are likely to spend a higher percentage of their income on 
housing, which can lead to increased financial strain. Housing is generally considered affordable when it 
accounts for no more than 30% of a household’s income; exceeding this threshold often forces families 
to make difficult choices between housing and other basic needs. 
How do HOME rents / Fair Market Rent compare to Area Median Rent? How 
might this impact your strategy to produce or preserve affordable housing? 
Both HOME rents and Fair Market Rents (FMR) are well below the area median rents in Cheyenne. This 
gap makes it more difficult to produce or preserve affordable housing without additional subsidies. 
Because rental subsidies tied to FMR or HOME limits may not be enough to secure units in the open 
market-especially with rents having increased 42% since 2010-low- and moderate-income households 
may have a harder time finding affordable options. 
Given that funding sources are limited, the City cannot fully close these funding gaps on its own. 
However, by removing barriers to affordable housing development and increasing the overall supply of 
housing, the City can help reduce housing costs in the long term. Other strategies include encouraging 
and supporting more development projects that use Low-Income Housing Tax Credits and utilizing CDBG 
funds to rehabilitate affordable housing projects. 
In summary, the difference between FMR/HOME rents and area median rents means that producing or 
preserving affordable housing will require additional financial support or policy interventions to keep 
rents affordable for those who need it most.

MA-20 Housing Market Analysis: Condition of Housing – 91.210(a) 
Introduction 
 
The City of Cheyenne defines “standard condition” housing as well-maintained, structurally sound 
homes meeting all local and HUD standards, while “substandard but suitable for rehabilitation” units are 
those that fall short of these criteria but can feasibly be improved through repairs. These definitions 
guide the city’s rehabilitation efforts and ensure compliance with building and fire codes. With only 20% 
of Cheyenne’s housing stock built since 2000, there is a significant need for both owner-occupied and 
rental unit rehabilitation. Additionally, a substantial portion of homes-especially those built before 1980-
pose potential lead-based paint hazards, with an estimated 355 low- or moderate-income units 
requiring testing and possible remediation. The overall condition of Cheyenne’s housing stock highlights 
the urgent need for targeted strategies to address rehabilitation needs and the removal of lead-based 
paint hazards. 
Describe the jurisdiction's definition of "standard condition" and "substandard condition but 
suitable for rehabilitation": 
The City’s Housing & Community Development (H&CD) Division defines “standard condition” and 
“substandard condition but suitable for rehabilitation” according to its Minimum Housing Rehabilitation 
Standards, which are based on the Uniform Building Code (UBC), Uniform Housing Codes, and follow 
HUD Section 8 Minimum Housing Quality Standards and other HUD guidelines. 
• 
Standard Condition: A unit in standard condition is a well-maintained residential structure built 
on a permanent, stable foundation. It must have solid structural integrity, be weather-tight and 
insulated against the elements, and include all basic mechanical systems: plumbing, electricity, 
heating, hot water, toilet facilities, and kitchen facilities, as required by the Uniform Building 
Code and HUD standards. 
• 
Substandard Condition (but Suitable for Rehabilitation): A unit is considered substandard if it 
lacks a permanent solid foundation, structural integrity, weather tightness, minimal insulation, 
or if its basic mechanical systems do not meet current standards. It may also show deferred 
maintenance to the extent that the structure is at risk of increased decay. However, if such a 
unit can feasibly be brought up to standard through rehabilitation, it is considered suitable for 
rehabilitation. 
These definitions serve as the minimum requirements for rehabilitation projects using HUD funding and 
are intended as guidance for contractors, developers, homeowners, and city officials. All rehabilitation 
work must also comply with requirements set by the City’s Building and Fire Departments before 
starting.

The CHA utilizes capital funds for ongoing modernization and preventive maintenance, including 
upgrades such as new flooring and electrical system improvements. The agency is committed to 
maintaining high occupancy rates and ensuring quick turnaround times for unit readiness. 
HUD contracts a third-party to conduct unit inspections in accordance with HUD’s NSPIRE standards. The 
most recent NSPIRE inspection for the CHA took place on October 24, 2024. During this inspection, HUD 
randomly selected 30 out of CHA’s 341 Public Housing units—266 located in Cheyenne and 75 in 
Laramie. CHA achieved a score of 90 during the October 24, 2024, inspection. Due to this high score, 
CHA’s Public Housing units will not require another HUD inspection for three years.

MA-25 Public and Assisted Housing – 91.210(b) 
Introduction 
The Cheyenne Housing Authority (CHA) owns and manages a variety of properties in Cheyenne and 
Laramie, including single-family homes and apartments. In Cheyenne, the CHA also offers apartment 
communities specifically designed to serve elderly and disabled individuals. All CHA Public Housing 
properties in Cheyenne and Laramie are smoke-free, ensuring a healthier environment for residents. 
These housing units are subsidized by the U.S. Department of Housing and Urban Development (HUD) 
and are maintained in full compliance with HUD regulations. The CHA continually accepts applications 
for its programs, including the Housing Choice Voucher Program—commonly known as Section 8. This 
federal program is the primary means by which very low-income households, as well as elderly and 
disabled persons, receive assistance to afford decent, safe, and sanitary housing in the private market 
and Qualified households participating in the Housing Choice Voucher Program receive vouchers to help 
subsidize their rent payments to landlords of privately owned properties. Participants are responsible 
for finding a suitable housing unit of their choice, provided the landlord agrees to participate in the 
program and the unit meets the CHA’s minimum health and safety standards. The CHA pays a housing 
subsidy directly to the landlord on behalf of the participating household, while the household pays the 
difference between the actual rent and the subsidized amount. 
Of the 316 residents occupying the 316 units that the CHA reported in use as of February 2025, 100% of 
those households requested accessibility features that were current residents of those units, 192 
reported being elderly or disabled. Waitlists are long and they have a limited number of accessible units. 
Data provided by the CHA in February 2025, provides information about the number of units in 
Cheyenne. Detailed information about the number of accessible units was unavailable.

Describe the supply of public housing developments:  
Describe the number and physical condition of public housing units in the 
jurisdiction, including those that are participating in an approved Public Housing 
Agency Plan: 
 Indian Hills Manor provides apartments for seniors and people with disabilities, while Golden Meadows 
offers income-restricted townhomes, and McGowen Apartments serve seniors aged 55 and older. WRAP 
Houses, designed for larger families of two to eight people, are also income restricted. As the primary 
provider of public housing in Cheyenne, the CHA faces high demand and long waitlists due to an ongoing 
shortage of affordable housing. CHA manages 266 public housing units, all of which participate in an 
approved Public Housing Agency (PHA) Plan and are maintained to meet HUD’s Uniform Physical 
Condition Standards (UPCS). To ensure units remain decent, safe, and sanitary, the CHA conducts move-
in, annual, preventative, and special inspections, while HUD’s Real Estate Assessment Center (REAC) may 
also randomly select units for inspection each year to verify compliance with federal standards.

Table 34 - Public Housing Condition 
 
Describe the restoration and revitalization needs of public housing units in the 
jurisdiction: 
The CHA’s public housing units require ongoing restoration and revitalization to remain safe, decent, 
and affordable for residents. The primary needs include capital improvements such as replacing roofs, 
windows, siding, and major building systems, as well as upgrades to flooring, sidewalks, and heating. 
Regular maintenance and prompt repairs are essential to address tenant requests, prevent 
deterioration, and ensure units meet safety and quality standards. 
A significant challenge is compliance with federal requirements, especially regarding lead-based paint 
hazards in older units. Cheyenne faces a shortage of certified inspectors and abatement professionals, 
which can slow down necessary rehabilitation and limit the number of units that can be improved each 
year. All restoration efforts must also bring units up to City and HUD minimum housing standards, 
ensuring structural integrity, weather-tightness, and the removal of hazardous materials.

Describe the public housing agency's strategy for improving the living 
environment of low- and moderate-income families residing in public housing: 
The CHA uses capital funds for ongoing modernization and preventive maintenance, including upgrades 
like new flooring and electrical system improvements, and aims to maintain high occupancy and quick 
turnaround times for unit readiness. 
HUD hires a third-party to perform unit inspections per HUD’s NSPIRE standards. The most recent 
NSPIRE inspection for the CHA was conducted on October 24, 2024. HUD randomly inspected 30 of the 
341 units in Cheyenne and Laramie; CHA has 341 Public Housing units, 266 in Cheyenne and 75 in 
Laramie. CHA’s score for the 10/24/2024 inspection was 90. Because of the high score, CHA’s Public

MA-30 Homeless Facilities and Services – 91.210(c)  
Introduction 
The Homeless Facilities and Services section provides an overview of the facilities and services currently 
available in Cheyenne to assist persons who are homeless. There are several programs in Cheyenne that 
provide shelter, transitional or temporary housing, rental assistance, and deposits to prevent or reduce 
homelessness. These programs operate shelters and two transitional housing facilities, provide 
stabilization and case management, and support clients working toward independent living. These 
agencies and programs work together to provide a continuum of care, from emergency shelter and 
healthcare to transitional housing, career support, and long-term stability services. By addressing both 
immediate needs and the root causes of homelessness, these coordinated efforts help individuals and 
families in Cheyenne move toward stable, independent living.  
The Wyoming Continuum of Crae reports that Cheyenne currently has 92 Year-round emergency shelter 
beds, 34 Transitional Housing Beds and 62 Permanent Supportive Housing Beds. The Table below 
provides a breakdown of some of the populations served, including Households with Children, 
Households with Only Adults and Veterans.

Describe mainstream services, such as health, mental health, and employment 
services to the extent those services are used to complement services targeted to 
homeless persons 
 A wide range of agencies and programs in Cheyenne provide mainstream services that 
complement targeted assistance for homeless individuals, including chronically homeless 
persons, families with children, veterans and their families, and unaccompanied youth. 
• 
Community Action of Laramie County (CALC): CALC offers intensive case management, 
mental and physical health services, and support aimed at helping clients achieve self-
sufficiency. CALC’s Homeless Health Clinic delivers primary healthcare and telehealth 
services to address both immediate and long-term health needs for homeless individuals 
and families.  
• 
Wyoming Department of Health (WDH): WHD’s Communicable Disease Unit administers 
the HOPWA grant, which provides supportive services such as case management, medical 
transportation reimbursement, and nutrition support for persons with AIDS/HIV. 
Healthworks offers sliding scale healthcare, dental care, and prescriptions to low- and 
moderate-income individuals, including those experiencing homelessness. 
• 
Comea Shelter Services: Comea provides case management and limited transportation for 
anyone who is homeless or at high risk of homelessness. Safehouse has 42 beds available 
for those in crisis. Staff is on call 24 hours a day, 7 days a week. The on-call staff responds 
to any calls generated through the hot line, Laramie County Sheriff's Office, Cheyenne 
Police Department, Pine Bluffs Police Department and other partner agencies. The shelter 
is staffed with advocates at the house or on call 24/7. The shelter staff assist clients in 
preparing for self-sufficiency by making referrals for health, mental health, and 
employment services.  
• 
 Family Promise of Cheyenne: Family Promise focuses on families with children, offering 
prevention services, rental assistance, and case management to help families avoid 
homelessness or quickly regain stable housing.  
• 
Unaccompanied Student Initiative (USI): USI supports unaccompanied youth, particularly 
teens, by providing prevention services, rental assistance, and case management, 
enabling them to secure safe housing while continuing their education and working 
towards self-sufficiency. 
• 
Wyoming Department of Workforce Services: Workforce Services has several programs 
available for individuals looking for a job throughout the state, individuals who may be 
unemployed and for individuals who are currently working and need to report a workplace 
issue. 
• 
Volunteers of America (VOA): VOA delivers stabilization services and case management, 
including through the Supportive Services for Veteran Families (SSVF) program, which 
provides rental assistance, deposits, car repair, and connections to VA benefits. Recover 
Wyoming administers the federally funded PATH (Projects for Assistance in Transition from 
Homelessness) Program, serving individuals who are homeless or at risk and have a 

 
  Consolidated Plan 
CHEYENNE 
    82 
OMB Control No: 2506-0117 (exp. 09/30/2021) 
serious mental illness by offering case management, housing search support, and limited 
financial assistance for deposits or prevention. The local domestic violence shelter offers a 
Client Emergency Fund to cover security and utility deposits as well as the first month’s 
rent. 
• 
 Peak Wellness Center Transitions Program: Peak Wellness Center supports homeless and 
low-income men in Cheyenne with accessible, affordable mental health and substance 
abuse services, including treatment and detox for drug and alcohol addiction, as well as 
buprenorphine services. They also assist youth and families with evaluation, prevention, 
diagnosis, and treatment of mental, emotional, and behavioral health issues, using 
psychotherapy and counseling to improve overall well-being. 
 
• 
Laramie County Community College’s Adult Career and Education System (ACES): The 
ACES program provides free instruction in digital literacy, high school equivalency, and 
English as a second language, collaborating with USI to support homeless clients in 
advancing their education and careers. Climb Wyoming assists single low- to moderate-
income mothers, including those experiencing homelessness, by helping them earn 
certifications, build resumes, prepare for interviews, and transition to new careers. 
 
• 
 Habitat for Humanity’s Fresh Start Program: Fresh Start offers vouchers for individuals 
facing difficult situations, such as transitioning out of homelessness, recovering from 
addiction, or escaping domestic violence, which can be used at the ReStore to purchase 
essential furniture. 
 
• 
I Am My Brother and My Sister’s Keeper (IAMBSK): IAMBSK provides meals, clothing, and 
other essentials to homeless individuals, further supporting this vulnerable population. 
Together, these agencies and programs create a comprehensive network of support for 
people experiencing homelessness in Cheyenne.

facilities are listed on screen  
There are several programs in Cheyenne that provide shelter, transitional or temporary housing, rental 
assistance, and deposits to prevent or reduce homelessness. 
• 
Volunteers of America Northern Rockies (VOA): VOA operates two transitional housing facilities 
in Cheyenne. One focuses on individuals in crisis, providing stabilization and case management, 
while the other, Mirror Lake Manor, supports clients working toward independent living. 
Residents typically stay about six months as they develop skills for independence. 

 
  Consolidated Plan 
CHEYENNE 
    83 
OMB Control No: 2506-0117 (exp. 09/30/2021) 
• 
Community Action of Laramie County (CALC): CALC runs Smith Manor, which offers twelve 
transitional housing units for men and women re-entering the workforce, and six apartments for 
individuals with disabilities. CALC also provides single-occupancy and family transitional housing, 
with case management and affordable rent for income-eligible families. CALC partners with the 
VA Medical Center and Veteran Housing Services on the Bridge Housing Program, helping low-
income homeless veterans and their families regain stability and move toward independence. 
• 
Comea Shelter Services: Comea offers two transitional programs: The Transitional Living 
Program (TLP): A pay-to-stay program for individuals, lasting from six to 24 months. The Journey 
Center: A 90-day program for families with minor children, focusing on securing employment 
and housing. 
• 
Family Promise of Cheyenne: Family Promise provides shelter for families in crisis, partnering 
with local churches to offer safe places to stay. They also offer prevention services, rental 
assistance, and case management to help families achieve economic stability and avoid 
homelessness. 
• 
Unaccompanied Student Initiative (USI): Provides a stable home for homeless teens that live in a 
variety of unsafe, temporary situations - including cars, parks, shelters, motels, and couch 
surfing. 
• 
Wyoming Department of Health, Communicable Disease Unit: HOPWA also offers programs like 
short-term rental, mortgage, and utility assistance (STRMU), tenant-based rental assistance 
(TBRA), permanent housing placement (PHP), and emergency shelter, which provides temporary 
motel stays while clients secure stable housing.

Community-Based Services

Humankind: 24/7 in-home and community-based support for individuals with intellectual and

Volunteers of America Northern Rockies (VOANR) 
• 
Transitional Housing & Crisis Stabilization: VOANR operates transitional living facilities in 
Cheyenne, such as Mirror Lake Manor, which provides supported living for individuals with 
mental health needs. Residents typically stay for about six months, working with clinicians and 
case managers on independence skills before moving to long-term housing. Serenity Place offers 
crisis stabilization, serving as an entry point for those in acute need and helping them transition 
toward stability and community living. 
• 
Long-Term and Supportive Housing: Facilities like Creekside Cottage offer therapeutic group 
living for those with severe and persistent mental illness, focusing on skill development, 
education, and community integration. Other supportive housing options allow for more 
independent living with case management and mental health plan support. 
• 
Veteran Services: VOANR also provides housing and case management for veterans, including 
rent and deposit assistance, connection to VA benefits, and support for those at risk of 
homelessness. 
Comea House & Resource Center 
• 
Emergency and Transitional Shelter: Comea offers emergency shelter for up to 7 days, including 
meals, hygiene, and case management. For those needing longer-term support, the Journey 
Program provides structured pathways from stabilization to employment and eventual 
independent housing. The Pay-to-Stay program offers semi-private living for up to 120 days, 
with continued case management and support for mental health and self-sufficiency.

Self-Sufficiency Transitional Housing: CALC offers single-occupancy and family apartments

During the next program year, the City will address the supportive service needs of Cheyenne residents 
who are not homeless but have other special needs through targeted activities funded by HUD CDBG 
and local matching funds. A total of $62,416 in CDBG funds and $37,584 in local match will be allocated 
to five nonprofit agencies to support public service programs that align with the City’s Consolidated Plan 
goals to promote self-sufficiency and provide job training opportunities.  
The specific funded projects for the 2025 Program Year include: 
• 
Meals On Wheels: More than a Meal for Laramie County Seniors 
Funding: $12,483 CDBG + $7,514 City match = $20,000 
This project will provide meals to individuals over age 65 living below the poverty line and 
receiving SNAP benefits, helping their SNAP funds last longer each month. The funding will also 
support expanded outreach to connect additional eligible community members to the program. 
• 
Healthworks: Enhancing Access to Healthcare with Interpretation Services 
Funding: $12,483 CDBG + $7,514 City match = $20,000 
This initiative will implement comprehensive professional interpretation services at 
Healthworks, covering a broader range of languages and introducing video conferencing 
capabilities. This will enable approximately 175 patients annually with limited English proficiency 
to communicate directly with providers during medical, behavioral health, and case 
management appointments, ensuring equitable access to care. 
• 
Climb Wyoming Job Training Program 
Funding: $12,483 CDBG + $7,514 City match = $20,000

CDBG funds will support free job training and counseling for low-income single mothers in 
Cheyenne, empowering them to increase their earning potential and achieve self-sufficiency. 
This program removes financial barriers to workforce development and helps families build 
more stable futures. 
• 
LCCC ACES Program – Digital Literacy 
Funding: $12,483 CDBG + $7,514 City match = $20,000 
The Laramie County Community College ACES Program will provide digital literacy instruction to 
approximately 150 low-to-moderate income Cheyenne residents, supporting the City’s annual 
goal to expand job training and employment opportunities. 
These activities directly support the City’s one-year goals to promote self-sufficiency, enhance access to 
supportive services, and provide job training for residents with special needs who are not homeless. 
For entitlement/consortia grantees: Specify the activities that the jurisdiction plans to

identified in accordance with 91.215(e) with respect to persons who are not homeless but

During the 2025 Program Year, the Housing & Community Development (H&CD) Division will launch a 
supplemental mini grant cycle. This initiative will provide up to $259,000 in funding for shovel-ready 
infrastructure and public facility projects that directly benefit low- and moderate-income (LMI) 
households and serve vulnerable populations. The mini grant cycle is designed to support projects that 
align with the 2025-2027 Con Plan goals of Preserving and Improving Low-Income Neighborhoods and 
Public Facility Improvements. 
To maximize impact, H&CD staff have proactively conducted outreach to educate nonprofits about 
Community Development Block Grant (CDBG) eligible activities and to identify high-impact projects that 
address the needs of non-homeless individuals with special needs. These efforts are directly linked to 
our one-year goals of improving access to quality housing and supportive services for LMI residents and 
other vulnerable groups.

MA-40 Barriers to Affordable Housing – 91.210(e)  
Describe any negative effects of public policies on affordable housing and 
residential investment. 
Wyoming faces a critical shortage of nonprofit developers and limited funding for affordable housing. 
The state must add tens of thousands of new homes by 2030 to meet projected demand, but the 
current shortfall is especially severe for extremely low-income households, who have far fewer 
affordable and available rental homes. This disproportionately impacts extremely low-income residents. 
According to the National Low Income Housing Coalition 2025 Wyoming Housing Profile- only 52 rental 
homes are affordable and available for every 100 extremely low-income household and 72% of 
extremely low-income renters are severely cost-burdened. Through consultation with affordable 
housing developers and as part of the updated 2025 Analysis to Impediments to Fair Housing Study, 
Wyoming’s affordable housing sector has identified several significant barriers that hinder the 
development of new affordable homes, particularly for extremely low-income households. 
Wyoming lacks both a State Revolving Loan Fund and a State Housing Trust Fund, making it one of only a 
few states without such resources. A recent legislative effort to create a State Housing Trust Fund with 
substantial annual funding did not advance. This fund would have supported new construction, 
rehabilitation, maintenance, and homelessness initiatives, and included a revolving loan component at 
or below market rates, as well as capacity-building for affordable housing development. 
Six bills were introduced during the 2025 session to simplify housing development by removing barriers 
or establishing funding, but none passed. For example, House Bill 68 (HB0068), which would have 
supported tax increment financing for affordable housing by expanding the Wyoming Urban Renewal 
Code, died in committee. Another attempt to amend the Zoning Protest Petition law—which allows a 
small percentage of nearby property owners to force a supermajority city council vote on 
developments—also failed. This law recently blocked a large apartment project and left the site vacant 
during a period of high demand. 
Developers struggle to make projects financially feasible, particularly due to infrastructure costs. 
Wyoming’s infrastructure funding, managed by the State Lands and Investment Board (SLIB), is 
accessible only to municipalities, not private developers or nonprofits. Wyoming’s state constitution 
restricts funding to nonprofits except for direct aid to the poor, which does not include affordable 
housing development. As a result, when municipalities need to apply for the funds, projects are subject 
to public processes where resident opposition can halt developments. Nonprofits and developers may 
apply for federal funding through state-administered programs, but these resources are limited, highly 
competitive. 

 
  Consolidated Plan 
CHEYENNE

MA-45 Non-Housing Community Development Assets – 91.215 (f) 
Introduction 
Cheyenne’s location at the intersection of I-80 and I-25 in southeastern Wyoming position the City as a 
regional center for employment and business activity. The city has seen an increase in new businesses 
and development, resulting in a range of job opportunities. According to recent data, the largest 
business sectors are education and health, finance, insurance and real estate, and arts, entertainment, 
and accommodations-including gambling facilities. Together, these sectors account for 43% of jobs in 
Cheyenne, and new venues such as Horse Palace Swan Ranch are projected to add more than 140 jobs. 
Job growth is expected in these sectors as well as in construction, indicating a continued need for skilled 
workers. Efforts to support job training programs, job placement services, and infrastructure 
improvements are likely to play a role in meeting workforce demands and maintaining economic 
stability. These initiatives may help workers develop the skills needed for available jobs, but challenges 
remain in ensuring that growth is balanced and benefits are widely shared across the community. 
The labor market in Cheyenne shows considerable variation in wages and job distribution. Higher 
average hourly wages are found in management, healthcare, and engineering, while lower wages are 
common in personal care, food service, and maintenance positions. Most employment is concentrated 
on office support, transportation, and sales, with fewer jobs in creative or specialized fields. A notable 
gender wage gap exists, with women earning less than men at all education levels and employment 
types. Addressing this disparity, along with supporting workforce development for underrepresented 
groups, remains an ongoing concern. Cheyenne also has a relatively short average commute, which may 
contribute to a less burdensome daily travel experience compared to national averages.

CHEYENNE 
    94 
OMB Control No: 2506-0117 (exp. 09/30/2021) 
Based on the Business Activity Table data available in the 2016-202 ACS and the 2023 ACS 1-Year 
Estimate data sets, the largest employment sector in Cheyenne is the Education and Health sectors, with 
approximately 8,245 workers according to the 2023 ACS 1-Year Estimates. 
Major employers for this Sector are: 
• 
Laramie County School District #1, Laramie County Community College 
• 
Cheyenne Regional Medical Center, which has over 2,000 employees and more than 170 active 
associate medical staff. 
The city also has 517 active physicians and numerous healthcare facilities, such as UCHealth Primary 
Care, Cheyenne Children’s Clinic, Health First, Cheyenne Plaza Primary Care, the Cheyenne VA Medical 
Center, the Laramie County Department of Health, the Wyoming Department of Health, and the 90th 
Medical Group at Warren Air Base. 
The sector’s size is driven by these large institutions, Cheyenne’s role as a regional hub, and the stability 
of public funding, offering diverse job opportunities with competitive wages and benefits and being less 
affected by economic fluctuations than other industries. 
Other major business sectors include Finance, Insurance, and Real Estate (2,876 jobs), which provides 
essential services to Cheyenne’s growing population and business community, supporting steady job 
growth. 
The Arts, Entertainment, and Accommodations sector employs 2,745 people, with a significant portion 
of jobs linked to gambling facilities such as the Horse Palace and Wyoming Downs; new venues like 
Horse Palace Swan Ranch are projected to add over 140 jobs. 
Together, these three business sectors account for 43% of jobs in Cheyenne. 
Describe the workforce and infrastructure needs of the business community: 
The projected job growth in Wyoming, especially in construction, health care, hospitality, and retail, 
shows that Cheyenne’s businesses need more skilled workers in these areas. Local employers are 
looking for people with technical skills and training to fill these jobs and help their companies grow. 
Many available jobs require at least a high school diploma or some technical education. Continued 
investment in things like business parks, fiber optic networks, and dependable utilities supports business 
growth and makes it easier for workers to get to their jobs. Supporting job training programs, job 
placement services, and infrastructure improvements will help the city’s workforce and economy stay 
strong and competitive. 

 
  Consolidated Plan 
CHEYENNE 
    95 
OMB Control No: 2506-0117 (exp. 09/30/2021) 
Describe any major changes that may have an economic impact, such as planned 
local or regional public or private sector investments or initiatives that have 
affected or may affect job and business growth opportunities during the planning 
period. Describe any needs for workforce development, business support or 
infrastructure these changes may create. 
Cheyenne’s major infrastructure investments, such as the new Meta data center and upgrades to roads, 
utilities, and public facilities, will create important needs for workforce development, business support, 
and continued infrastructure improvements during the 2025–2027 planning period. The Meta data 
center project alone will generate over 1,000 construction jobs and about 100 permanent positions, 
increasing demand for skilled workers in construction, technology, and support services. To meet this 
demand, the city will need more training programs, apprenticeships, and partnerships with local schools 
and colleges to help residents gain the skills needed for these new jobs. 
Local businesses will also need support to take advantage of the growth opportunities that come with 
better infrastructure and increased economic activity. This may include help with business planning, 
expansion, and navigating new regulations. Organizations like Cheyenne LEADS will be important in 
guiding both new and existing businesses through these changes. 
Finally, as the city grows, there will be a need for ongoing investment in public facilities, transportation, 
utilities, and safety services to keep up with increased use and to maintain a high quality of life for 
residents. Funding for these improvements will likely come from a mix of local taxes, grants, and 
partnerships with private companies. Overall, Cheyenne’s investments will require a focused effort on 
workforce training, business support, and infrastructure planning to ensure the city’s growth is 
successful and sustainable through 2027. 
How do the skills and education of the current workforce correspond to 
employment opportunities in the jurisdiction? 
The top occupations by projected growth from Wyoming Labor Force March 2025 Trends include 
construction laborers, general and operations managers, retail salespersons, and stockers/order fillers. 
Most of these roles require no more than a high school diploma, though some, such as general and 
operations managers and truck drivers, require post-secondary credentials. Notably, occupations with 
no formal education requirement account for about one-third of all job openings, while those requiring 
a high school diploma make up the largest share (38.6%). 

 
  Consolidated Plan 
CHEYENNE 
    96 
OMB Control No: 2506-0117 (exp. 09/30/2021) 
Describe any current workforce training initiatives, including those supported by 
Workforce Investment Boards, community colleges and other organizations. 
Describe how these efforts will support the jurisdiction's Consolidated Plan. 
Wyoming is launching several workforce training programs to help residents gain new skills, meet 
industry needs, and create more job opportunities. These programs are supported by Workforce 
Investment Boards, community colleges, and other organizations in the state. 
The Wyoming Department of Workforce Services runs programs through One-Stop Workforce Centers. 
These centers offer skills assessments, job planning, career counseling, occupational training, on-the-job 
training, and work experience. The programs focus on fields like healthcare, construction, 
transportation, environmental technology, and office administration. They also provide support such as 
financial literacy and soft skills training to help people find and keep jobs. 
The Workforce Development Training Fund offers grants to Wyoming businesses and workers for 
professional development and customized training. These grants help businesses improve employee 
skills, develop apprenticeships, and offer internships to meet changing industry needs and keep talent in 
the state. 
Laramie County Community College offers a wide range of workforce training options, including 
apprenticeships, health and safety certifications, leadership training, and programs for local industries. 
LCCC provides short-term career training in high-demand fields, technical and trades programs, business 
and professional development courses, and customized training for businesses. The college also 
supports apprenticeship programs, dual enrollment for students, and extra support services. 
Wyoming has also started Next Generation Sector Partnerships, which bring together employers, 
educators, and public agencies to identify workforce gaps and create training programs. These 
partnerships are active in construction, hospitality, and new industries, and have helped increase 
training and improve job outcomes.

MA-60 Broadband Needs of Housing occupied by Low- and Moderate-
Income Households - 91.210(a)(4), 91.310(a)(2) 
 
Describe the need for broadband wiring and connections for households, 
including low- and moderate-income households and neighborhoods. 
Broadband access is a critical necessity for households in Cheyenne, WY, including those with low and 
moderate incomes. Reliable internet connectivity enables access to essential services such as telehealth, 
remote education, and economic opportunities like job searches and remote work. It also plays a key 
role in promoting educational equity by providing students with access to online learning resources and 
supporting healthcare through telemedicine options. However, significant barriers remain, particularly 
for underserved neighborhoods, due to affordability challenges and infrastructure gaps. 
Efforts to address these issues include initiatives like federal investments through Connect Wyoming and 
local provider programs aimed at expanding broadband infrastructure and affordability. Notably, the 
Affordable Connectivity Program (ACP) provided financial assistance to low-income households by 
subsidizing internet costs and offering discounts on devices. However, the ACP ended in June 2024 due 
to funding constraints, leaving a gap in support for some LMI families. 
On April 26, 2025, the Housing & Community Development Manager consulted Chad Bolling, Wyoming's 
Broadband Manager, to discuss the pressing need for broadband connections in Cheyenne. Bolling 
emphasized that improved broadband access would directly benefit households by enabling remote 
work, education, telehealth services, and health monitoring for vulnerable populations. 
Describe the need for increased competition by having more than one broadband 
Internet service provider serve the jurisdiction. 
The Wyoming State Broadband Program, administered by the Wyoming Business Council, is dedicated to 
ensuring that every resident and business in Wyoming has access to affordable, reliable, redundant, and 
future-proof broadband service. The program currently includes over fifty broadband providers 
statewide, all of which participate in the Affordability Connectivity Program. Through these efforts, the 
Broadband Program is actively working to improve both the connectivity and affordability of broadband 
for communities across Wyoming (WCDA 2023-2027 Con Plan). 
 
According to BestNeighborhood.org, Cheyenne is served by 12 residential internet providers, covering 
99.78% of the city. The average home in Cheyenne can access speeds up to 2,270 Mbps, with residents 
able to choose from between 1 and 10 providers depending on their location. Service types available 
include fixed wireless, DSL, cable, and fiber, though availability may vary by neighborhood. This level of 
provider diversity has led to a generally healthy competitive environment. 
 

 
  Consolidated Plan 
CHEYENNE 
    99 
OMB Control No: 2506-0117 (exp. 09/30/2021) 
The Wyoming Business Council’s Broadband Manager noted during consultation for the Con Plan that 
the ongoing need for increased competition among broadband providers in Cheyenne. Mr. Bolling 
emphasized that having multiple providers is essential for several reasons: 
 
• 
Lower Costs: Increased competition drives down prices, making internet service more affordable 
for consumers. 
• 
Improved Quality: Providers are incentivized to enhance their service quality and reliability to 
attract and retain customers. 
• 
Greater Accessibility: Competition encourages providers to expand their networks, reaching 
underserved or unserved areas and ensuring more equitable access. 
 
Cheyenne continues to invest in broadband infrastructure, with projects such as Visionary Broadband’s 
fiber expansion—funded by Wyoming Business Council grants—aiming to address connectivity gaps in 
underserved neighborhoods. While these initiatives are crucial, fostering robust competition among 
providers remains a key strategy for ensuring that all residents and businesses benefit from affordable, 
high-speed internet. 
 
While Cheyenne currently enjoys a relatively competitive broadband market, continued efforts to 
support and attract multiple providers are vital. Increased competition will not only lower costs and 
improve service quality but will also ensure that the internet is accessible to everyone, supporting the 
city’s economic growth and quality of life.

MA-65 Hazard Mitigation - 91.210(a)(5), 91.310(a)(3) 
 
Describe the jurisdiction’s increased natural hazard risks associated with climate change. 
The Housing & Community Development Division collaborated with Cheyenne Fire Rescue and the 
Department of Engineering to assess natural hazard risks in Cheyenne linked to climate change. Kip 
Sanders, Administrative Battalion Chief, provided two key reports: a Wildland Incident Heatmap for 
2024 and a Wildland Incident by Month chart for 2024. These reports highlight that the primary natural 
hazards tracked by Cheyenne Fire Rescue are extreme cold/winter storms and wildfires. However, 
winter storms are not specifically monitored, as emergency calls are coded based on call type rather 
than weather events. 
The wildfire data for 2024 reveals eight incidents across Cheyenne, with no single area being more 
susceptible than others. The monthly incident chart shows a notable spike in wildfires from April to May, 
doubling in frequency before declining in subsequent months. This trend underscores the seasonal 
nature of wildfire risks. The heatmap illustrates the geographic distribution of these incidents, 
emphasizing the widespread nature of wildfire threats across the city. 
Additionally, the H&CD Staff consulter with the City’s Stormwater Division Manager and City Drainage 
Engineer about the City’s strategies for Hazard mitigation. There are potential increases in natural 
hazards in and around Cheyenne can most readily be seen through the increases in observed rainfall 
intensities and the associated statistical predictions used in modelling rainfall events. Two separate 
studies, 37 years apart, clearly demonstrate this. 
 Figures 1 and 2 (below) illustrate the differences between rainfall intensities from a study completed in 
the late eighties (USGS) versus a study completed in the early 2020’s (NOAA). A comparison of the study 
results shows that rainfall intensities for shorter duration rainstorms have significantly increased over 
the last four decades. This trend is prevalent for all standard design storms (2-year through 100-year 
events).  The implications of these increases will lead to more localized flooding problems, as more 

 
  Consolidated Plan 
CHEYENNE 
    101 
OMB Control No: 2506-0117 (exp. 09/30/2021) 
stormwater runoffs will be generated with more frequent storm events. 
 

 
  Consolidated Plan 
CHEYENNE 
    102 
OMB Control No: 2506-0117 (exp. 09/30/2021) 
 
 The implications of these increases will lead to more localized flooding problems, as more stormwater 
runoffs will be generated with more frequent storm events. This will be especially prevalent in older 
areas of Cheyenne, which are typically occupied by low to moderate income households, where storm 
sewer systems are consistently undersized or non-existent.  Figure 3 (attached) shows two locations in 
Cheyenne that will experience the impacts due to insufficient storm sewer collection infrastructure. 
Describe the vulnerability to these risks of housing occupied by low- and moderate-income 
households based on an analysis of data, findings, and methods. 
While Cheyenne Fire Rescue does not conduct risk assessments based on socioeconomic data, 
demographic trends are considered broadly. Mitigation efforts have focused on city-owned properties 
identified as high-risk for wildfires, but addressing risks on private property remains challenging due to 
the need for owner cooperation and associated costs. Climate change is expected to exacerbate these 
hazards. Rising temperatures and prolonged droughts in Wyoming are increasing wildfire frequency and 
severity, as well as reducing snowpack critical for water resources. These changes heighten risks to 
property, health, and ecosystems while complicating mitigation efforts.

Strategic Plan 
SP-05 Overview 
This section presents the City of Cheyenne’s strategic approach to funding, emphasizing priorities such 
as low- and moderate-income (LMI) census tracts and block groups, which will receive the majority of 
Community Development Block Grant (CDBG) resources. It outlines the strategies designed to address 
the City’s most pressing needs, identifies the populations and areas targeted, and explains the rationale 
behind these priorities. 
The Strategic Plan Overview also details the City’s 2025–2027 strategies to: 
• 
Remove barriers to affordable housing 
• 
Address homelessness 
• 
Reduce lead hazards 
• 
Coordinate with the Cheyenne Housing Authority 
Additionally, the plan describes the City’s efforts to combat poverty and outlines strategies for effective 
monitoring of sub-recipients. 
Anticipated CDBG Resources 
This section specifies the actual U.S. Department of Housing & Urban Development (HUD) allocation for 
the 2025 Program Year, as well as other matching funds provided by the City for public service activities. 
Returned funds from previous grant cycles will be allocated to infrastructure and public facility projects. 
The Housing & Community Development Division (H&CD), which administers the CDBG grant, intends to 
expend all or most of the returned funds by June 30, 2026. 
To achieve this, the H&CD Division will open a supplemental or mini grant cycle before the end of the 
year. This mini grant cycle will fund shovel-ready projects that align with the Consolidated Plan goals of 
Preserving and Improving Low-Income Neighborhoods or Public Facility Improvements. Applications that 
include projects that are at least halfway completed, or will be finished by June 15, 2026, will be 
prioritized for funding. 
All funded activities must meet the requirements outlined in the Strategic Plan sections: 
• 
SP-25 (Priority Needs) 
• 
SP-35 (Anticipated Resources) 
• 
SP-45 (Goals Summary) 
 

 
  Consolidated Plan

    105 
OMB Control No: 2506-0117 (exp. 09/30/2021) 
The City’s 2025–2027 Con Plan identifies several priority needs in Section SP-25 to address housing, 
public service, infrastructure and public facility needs. All priority needs included in the Con Plan are 
considered “high” level needs. By strategically allocating CDBG funds to these priorities, the City’s goal is  
to maximize the impact of its limited federal resources, serve the greatest number of low- and 
moderate-income residents, and ensure full compliance with HUD regulations.

General Allocation Priorities 
Describe the basis for allocating investments geographically within the jurisdiction (or within the 
EMSA for HOPWA) 
The City’s CDBG Target Area encompasses the entire city, with funds distributed citywide. For the 2025-
2027 Consolidated Plan, projects will focus on qualified census tracts and block groups that benefit low- 
to moderate-income (LMI) residents. Most Public Service activities will serve LMI individuals throughout 
Cheyenne. 
During this period, the City will prioritize infrastructure and public facility improvement projects and 
area benefit projects in qualified census tracts and LMI block groups to meet HUD’s area benefit 
requirements, ensuring that at least 51% of beneficiaries are LMI residents in primarily residential areas.

• 
Census Tract 15, Block Groups 2 (70.60% LMI) & 3 (64.11% LMI): Northeastern Cheyenne 
• 
Census Tract 2, Block Group 1 (85.59% LMI) & Block Group 2 (65.05% LMI): South Cheyenne 
• 
Census Tract 6, Block Group 4 (55.74% LMI): East of Downtown 
• 
Census Tract 7, Block Group 1 (65.75% LMI): Downtown and west of downtown Cheyenne 
By targeting these LMI areas, the City of Cheyenne ensures that CDBG resources address key community 
needs and maximize benefits for LMI residents, in alignment with HUD regulations.

Description 
 
Provide improvements and rehabilitation to public facilities, prioritizing 
transitional housing, housing for special needs populations and shelters, to 
enhance their functionality and support for individuals and families in need. 
Basis for 
Relative 
Priority 
Improving and rehabilitating public facilities, including transitional housing, 
housing for special needs populations, and shelters, is a top priority in response 
to the growing demand for services that address homelessness and housing 
instability. Local organizations such as the Comea Shelter, Unaccompanied 
Student Initiative, the local domestic violence shelter, Community Action of 
Laramie County, and Accessible Space Inc. have all reported increasing strain on 
their resources as they provide emergency shelter, transitional housing, 
affordable housing and supportive services to vulnerable groups, including 
individuals with disabilities, families, and people recovering from addiction. 
The shortage of affordable housing and supportive infrastructure further 
exacerbates the challenges faced by low- and extremely low-income residents, as 
well as those at risk of homelessness. By improving these facilities, agencies are 
better equipped to meet the community’s needs, promote self-sufficiency, and 
offer a pathway to stable housing for individuals and families in crisis.

Description 
CDBG funds will be allocated to support public services in three key areas: 
• 
Domestic Violence Shelter: Funding will assist with utility reimbursement 
to ensure the shelter can continue providing essential services to 
survivors. 
• 
Health Clinic: Resources will support language interpretation services, 
improving access to healthcare for low- and moderate-income (LMI) 
clients. 
• 
Senior Nutrition: Funds will be used to provide nutritious meals for LMI 
seniors, addressing their dietary needs and promoting overall well-being. 
Basis for 
Relative 
Priority 
Funding for Supportive (Public) Services for Low-Income and Special Needs 
populations is crucial for Cheyenne’s LMI residents, as these services ensure 
access to necessities such as healthcare, food, and shelter. Public services act as a 
vital safety net, helping to mitigate risks to health, safety, and stability for the 
city’s most vulnerable populations. Without adequate funding, individuals face 
increased risks of poor outcomes, including untreated health issues and 
homelessness, which can also result in higher societal costs. The City allocates 
15% of each year’s annual HUD funding to support these essential public 
services. For the 2025 Program Year, the Housing & Community Development 
Advisory Council reviewed twelve applications and recommended funding for 
five organizations with strong community partnerships, experience managing 
federal grants, and a proven track record of effective outcomes.

Program administration is essential to the effective operation of the CDBG 
program. Administrative funds cover critical tasks such as compliance 
monitoring, financial oversight, planning, and reporting, ensuring that activities 
align with federal regulations and community priorities. The City utilizes 20% of 
the annual CDBG allocation to cover these costs, which include staff salaries and 
operational costs. Without this support, the City lacks the resources necessary to 
implement and oversee projects, jeopardizing its ability to meet HUD standards 
and address local housing and community development needs effectively.

SP-30 Influence of Market Conditions – 91.215 (b) 
Influence of Market Conditions 
 
Affordable 
Housing Type 
Market Characteristics that will influence  
the use of funds available for housing type 
Tenant Based 
Rental Assistance 
(TBRA) 
Cost burden is the most significant housing challenges in Cheyenne, with a 
substantial proportion of renter-occupied households spending more than 30% 
of their income on housing. The high demand for affordable rental options, 
reflected in long Housing Choice Voucher waitlists, underscores the need for 
TBRA to help low-income households access and maintain stable housing.  
TBRA for Non-
Homeless Special 
Needs 
Rising area rents and lack of accessible units are making it increasingly difficult 
for Special Needs households to secure affordable rental housing. Additionally, 
the existing housing stock offers a limited number of units that can be feasibly 
rehabilitated for accessibility, as many older buildings are costly and 
challenging to modify to meet the needs of people with disabilities.  
New Unit 
Production 
The shortage of affordable housing units, rising construction costs, and limited 
availability of contractors willing or able to develop affordable homes have 
increased competition for available units, particularly among low- and 
moderate-income (LMI) households. New unit production is critical to 
expanding the housing supply and easing cost burdens across the community. 
There is also a growing need for accessible and affordable units designed for 
one to two occupants, reflecting the increasing senior population and their 
specific housing requirements. Cheyenne faces a significant shortage of 
accessible housing units to accommodate its aging population and residents 
with disabilities. According to data from the (ACS) 5-Year Estimates for 2019–
2023, Cheyenne has approximately 11,722 residents aged 65 and above, 
accounting for 18.04% of the city’s total population. This proportion is higher 
than the national average of 16.84% for the same period. Developing strategies 
to increase the number of accessible units is essential to meet the current and 
future housing needs of Cheyenne’s seniors and to 

 
  Consolidated Plan 
CHEYENNE 
    114 
OMB Control No: 2506-0117 (exp. 09/30/2021) 
Affordable 
Housing Type 
Market Characteristics that will influence  
the use of funds available for housing type 
Rehabilitation 
Rehabilitating Cheyenne’s housing stock is a crucial step in addressing the 
housing challenges faced by residents earning 0–80% of the Area Median 
Income (AMI). Deferred maintenance has contributed to the aging of many 
homes, and targeted improvements can significantly enhance energy efficiency, 
thereby reducing utility costs and easing the financial burden on LMI residents. 
These rehabilitation efforts help preserve existing affordable housing and 
improve long-term affordability for low-income households. 
However, retrofitting a large percentage of aging units does not always provide 
the level of accessibility required by all residents and, in many cases, serves 
only as a temporary solution. Therefore, prioritizing facility upgrades to, and 
the development of safe, affordable, and accessible housing units is essential. 
By sustaining and creating more suitable options, seniors can safely transition 
into housing that better meets their needs. This, in turn, allows younger 
families to purchase and customize existing homes, further supporting the 
community’s housing stability and diversity. 
 
Acquisition, 
including 
preservation 
Supporting acquisition and preservation helps maintain affordable 
homeownership and rental opportunities for current and future residents, 
especially in markets like Cheyenne with high-cost burdens and limited new 
supply. However, market factors such as the availability of suitable properties 
for acquisition, and the availability of organizations willing to invest in these 
efforts influence the feasibility of these efforts.

Public service

These investments are designed to fund activities that meet HUD requirements, address critical 
community needs, promote economic opportunity, and improve the quality of life for Cheyenne 
residents, especially LMI individuals and neighborhoods. The allocation process is guided by the Con

Service funds with $38,451 from City funds. This partnership will increase total public service funding to

The City Planning Department will utilize $250,000 in CDBG funding for major safety improvements 
through the Safe Routes to School Project at Alta Vista Elementary School, located in a low- and

Table 47 - Institutional Delivery Structure 
 
Explain the institutional structure through which the jurisdiction will carry out its 
consolidated plan including private industry, non-profit organizations, and public 
institutions. 
Cheyenne is fortunate to offer healthcare and workforce services through a strong network of 
organizations, including the Cheyenne Regional Medical Center, the Wyoming Department of Health, 
HealthWorks, and Crossroads Health Clinic. These providers offer general healthcare, HIV/AIDS care, 
mental health counseling, and mobile clinics, ensuring broad access to essential services. Workforce 
development is supported by Wyoming Workforce Services, Laramie County Community College, and 
programs like Climb Wyoming and NextGen, which focus on employment training and life skills. 
Mental health and substance abuse support are provided by agencies such as Recover Wyoming, the 
Cheyenne VA Medical Center Substance Abuse Treatment Program, Peak Wellness Center, and Harmony 
House. These organizations offer counseling and treatment, often targeting homeless individuals or 
those at risk. However, increasing demand and recent funding reductions have raised concerns about 
the continued availability of these critical services. 
The City is continually investing in neighborhood and infrastructure improvements through its Planning 
Department, Community Recreation and Events Department, Metropolitan Planning Organization, 
Downtown Development Authority, and Board of Public Utilities. Planned initiatives for 2025–2027 
include water infrastructure replacement, upgraded streets and sidewalks, facade improvements, 
enhancements to parks and greenways, and public facility upgrades, all targeted to support economic 
development and improve quality of life for Cheyenne residents. 
Cheyenne’s housing network is strengthened by collaboration among providers, City leadership, and 
organizations such as the Wyoming Housing Network, the Mayor’s Housing Task Force, Southeastern 

 
  Consolidated Plan 
CHEYENNE 
    119 
OMB Control No: 2506-0117 (exp. 09/30/2021) 
Wyoming Builders Association and the Laramie County Community Partnership Housing Force. 
Cheyenne Housing Authority, Wyoming Housing Network, Accessible Space.org and others manage 
affordable rental units, many of which participate in the Section 8 program.  
 Despite these efforts, challenges remain due to limited affordable housing, insufficient development 
funding, and inadequate resources for property maintenance. Continued collaboration and integrated 
efforts are essential to ensure all residents have access to safe housing and vital services. 
Availability of services targeted to homeless persons and persons with HIV and 
mainstream services 
 Cheyenne provides a range of targeted and mainstream services to homeless individuals, families, 
veterans, unaccompanied youth, and persons with HIV. Key providers such as Cheyenne Regional 
Hospital, the Wyoming Department of Health, HealthWorks, and Crossroads Health Clinic offer 
accessible healthcare, HIV/AIDS care, mental health counseling, and mobile clinics. Many of these 
services are specifically designed for or actively reach out to homeless populations and those at risk of 
homelessness. Employment and life skills support are available through Wyoming Workforce Services, 
Laramie County Community College, Climb Wyoming, and NextGen, helping homeless individuals and 
families gain job skills and improve self-sufficiency. Mental health and substance abuse treatment are 
provided by organizations like Recover Wyoming, the Cheyenne VA Medical Center Substance Abuse 
Treatment Program, Peak Wellness Center, and Harmony House. These programs serve chronically 
homeless individuals, families, veterans, youth, and persons with HIV. While these services are widely 
available and utilized, providers report increasing demand and some reductions in funding, which may 
impact ongoing access. Nonetheless, Cheyenne’s network of services remains a critical support for 
homeless persons and those with HIV.
