Table 48 - Homeless Prevention Services Summary 
 
Describe how the service delivery system, including, but not limited to, the 
services listed above meet the needs of homeless persons (particularly chronically 
homeless individuals and families, families with children, veterans and their 
families, and unaccompanied youth) 
Family Promise of Cheyenne and the local domestic violence shelter collaborate to support survivors of 
domestic violence and homeless families by providing both immediate shelter and assistance with 
longer-term housing needs. The Unaccompanied Student Initiative (USI), in partnership with the Laramie 
County School District and other local organizations, focuses on supporting homeless students. Beyond 
housing, USI offers transportation and academic support and connects students with case workers who 
help them prepare for further education, job training, internships, and independent living opportunities 
once they age out of the program. 
Community Action of Laramie County, the Comea Shelter, and the Cheyenne VA operate multiple 
programs designed to assist families, veterans and their families, and unaccompanied youth. These 
organizations work together to address the diverse needs of the homeless population through a variety 
of supportive services. 
However, there remains a significant programming gap for chronically homeless individuals, particularly 
those struggling with addiction, mental illness, or reentry after incarceration. The city currently lacks 
sufficient low-barrier shelter options for this population, making it challenging to provide consistent care 
and access to mental health services. Additional resources or program development may be needed to 
better address the needs of these individuals. 

 
  Consolidated Plan 
CHEYENNE 
    121 
OMB Control No: 2506-0117 (exp. 09/30/2021) 
Describe the strengths and gaps of the service delivery system for special needs 
population and persons experiencing homelessness, including, but not limited to, 
the services listed above 
There are many strengths and gaps in the service delivery system for special needs populations and 
individuals experiencing homelessness.  While there is a strong network of compassionate and 
knowledgeable agencies, there have historically been capacity challenges and organizations have, at 
times, been siloed, which could limit better outcomes for LMI populations. 
Wyoming211 has been a leader in serving as a referral center and resource hub for residents who are 
homeless or at risk of becoming homeless. However, a more comprehensive strategy is needed, 
particularly for services targeting people experiencing homelessness, to better leverage funding and 
resources, implement targeted strategies for specific populations, and improve data analysis and 
outcome tracking. Enhanced statewide communication and collaboration with the State Continuum of 
Care (CoC) is also necessary. The recent CoC three-part forum on gaps analysis brought these issues to 
the forefront, and strategies and benchmarks were identified to address deficiencies. 
 While Cheyenne is fortunate to have both a homeless shelter and a domestic violence shelter, these 
facilities are not considered low-barrier shelters. There is a pressing need for shelter options for 
homeless individuals with mental health or substance abuse issues, as well as for services and shelter for 
those exiting incarceration. Additionally, programs providing legal, mortgage, utility, and rental 
assistance need additional support, as these providers have reported an increase in requests for 
assistance. 
Provide a summary of the strategy for overcoming gaps in the institutional 
structure and service delivery system for carrying out a strategy to address 
priority needs 
The City has invested significant staff time in consulting and coordinating with members of the service 
delivery system to improve support for special needs populations, as well as individuals who are 
homeless or at risk of homelessness. The City actively participates in coordinated efforts such as the 
annual Point-in-Time Count, CoC grant review committees, housing and community development 
boards and task forces, and statewide associations such as WYNAHRO. These collaborations enable the 
City to work closely with service providers to address and overcome gaps in the institutional delivery 
system. 
Through the targeted allocation of CDBG funds, the City helps service providers address priority needs 
that impact these vulnerable populations. Funded initiatives include shelter utility support, 
improvements to transitional housing facilities, job training and digital literacy programs, meal and 
outreach services for seniors and persons with disabilities, and translation services to improve 
healthcare access.

SP-50 Public Housing Accessibility and Involvement – 91.215(c) 
Need to Increase the Number of Accessible Units (if Required by a Section 504 
Voluntary Compliance Agreement)  
The Cheyenne Housing Authority (CHA) has implemented various initiatives to address the needs of 
public housing effectively. The CHA is a recognized Public Housing Authority (PHA) that administers 
federal housing programs funded by the U.S. Department of Housing and Urban Development (HUD). 
While located within the City’s jurisdiction, CHA operates independently and is not managed by the City 
of Cheyenne. 
Each year, the CHA receives an annual allocation of Capital Funds from the HUD, which are utilized to 
maintain and improve public housing properties. These funds are competitively bid and allocated for 
essential upgrades and replacements, including roofing, siding, windows, flooring, cabinets, HVAC 
systems, concrete, and blacktop. This ongoing investment ensures that properties remain well-
maintained, operate efficiently, and adequately meet the housing needs of tenants. 
 
In addition to capital improvements, the CHA administers the Housing Choice Voucher (Section 8) 
program, which provides rental assistance to low-income families, the elderly, and individuals with 
disabilities. This program enables qualifying households to secure suitable housing in the private market 
while the CHA subsidizes a portion of the rent paid to landlords. Furthermore, the CHA operates 
specialized housing programs such as Indian Hills Manor, which offer apartments specifically designed 
for seniors aged 62 and older and individuals with disabilities. These targeted initiatives are crucial in 
addressing the unique housing requirements of vulnerable populations within the community. 
Activities to Increase Resident Involvements 
CHA has implemented strategies to encourage public housing residents to become more involved in 
management and participate in homeownership. Staff engage tenants in planning by inviting them to 
review and provide feedback on the agency's 5-year and Annual Plans. This initiative allows residents to 
voice their concerns and needs, which CHA considers when developing plans and allocating Capital 
Funds, ensuring that improvements align with the community's needs. 
 
Additionally, each multifamily public housing property has a Resident Council that provides a formal 
structure for tenant participation in management. These councils allow residents to collectively address 
concerns, propose ideas, and serve as liaisons between tenants and CHA administration.

Community Development Block Grant (CDBG) financial assistance is not being allocated to the CHA for 
the upcoming year.

SP-55 Barriers to affordable housing – 91.215(h)  
Barriers to Affordable Housing 
Wyoming faces a critical shortage of nonprofit developers and limited funding for affordable housing. 
The state must add tens of thousands of new homes by 2030 to meet projected demand, but the 
current shortfall is especially severe for extremely low-income households, who have far fewer 
affordable and available rental homes. This disproportionately impacts extremely low-income residents. 
According to the National Low Income Housing Coalition 2025 Wyoming Housing Profile- only 52 rental 
homes are affordable and available for every 100 extremely low-income households and 72% of 
extremely low-income renters are severely cost-burdened. Through consultation with affordable 
housing developers and as part of the updated 2025 Analysis to Impediments to Fair Housing Study, 
Wyoming’s affordable housing sector has identified several significant barriers that hinder the 
development of new affordable homes, particularly for extremely low-income households. 
Wyoming lacks both a State Revolving Loan Fund and a State Housing Trust Fund, making it one of only a 
few states without such resources. A recent legislative effort to create a Wyoming State Housing Trust 
Fund with substantial annual funding did not advance. This fund would have supported new 
construction, rehabilitation, maintenance, and homelessness initiatives, and included a revolving loan 
component at or below market rates, as well as capacity-building for affordable housing development. 
Six bills were introduced during the 2025 session to simplify housing development by removing barriers 
or establishing funding, but none passed. For example, House Bill 68 (HB0068), which would have 
supported tax increment financing for affordable housing by expanding the Wyoming Urban Renewal 
Code, died in committee. Another attempt to amend the Zoning Protest Petition law—which allows a 
small percentage of nearby property owners to force a supermajority city council vote on 
developments—also failed. This law recently blocked a large apartment project in Cheyenne and left the 
site vacant during a period of high demand. 
 
Developers struggle to make projects financially feasible, particularly due to infrastructure costs. 
Wyoming’s infrastructure funding, managed by the State Lands and Investment Board (SLIB), is 
accessible only to municipalities, not private developers or nonprofits. Wyoming’s state constitution 
restricts funding to nonprofits except for direct aid to the poor, which does not include affordable 
housing development. As a result, when municipalities need to apply for the funds, projects are subject 
to public processes where resident opposition can halt developments. Nonprofits and developers may 
apply for federal funding through state-administered programs, but these resources are limited and 
highly competitive. 

 
 
Annual Action Plan 
2025 
127 
OMB Control No: 2506-0117 (exp. 09/30/2021) 
Strategy to Remove or Ameliorate the Barriers to Affordable Housing 
The City’s strategy to remove barriers to affordable housing during the 2025–2027 Con Plan period 
includes the following actions: 
To address infrastructure barriers, the City will: 
1. Continue seeking funding opportunities, such as SLIB Infrastructure funding, to support 
affordable housing development. For example, the City provided infrastructure for the Habitat 
for Humanity Pronghorn Crossing Development, which includes 14 units. 
2. Provide regular updates to State legislators on Cheyenne’s barriers to affordable housing 
development and advocate for policies and funding that support affordable housing and 
economic development. 
3. Work with the Wyoming Business Council to amend their eligibility requirements for the 
Business Ready Grant and Loan program to include workforce housing. 
To address limited developer capacity, the City will: 
1. Continue exploring strategies to streamline local permitting and zoning processes. 
2. Further develop public engagement tools to educate the community about city projects and 
initiatives, and provide clear, consistent information to developers. 
3. Collaborate with state agencies to provide gap funding. 
To improve access to affordable housing, the City will: 
1. Utilize CDBG funds to support infrastructure and facility upgrades for transitional housing, as 
well as housing for seniors and vulnerable populations. 
2. Participate in long-term planning meetings for affordable housing projects involving multiple 
stakeholders, with a focus on developing housing for vulnerable populations such as seniors and 
persons with disabilities. 
3. Explore innovative collaborations, such as the Hope Springs Development Project in Greeley, 
CO—a partnership between Habitat, the City, the County, and private developers—which will 
provide 491 units on 42 acres using LIHTC.

SP-60 Homelessness Strategy – 91.215(d) 
Reaching out to homeless persons (especially unsheltered persons) and assessing 
their individual needs 
The H&CD Division staff has adopted a proactive approach to engaging homeless individuals, particularly 
those who are unsheltered, and assessing their unique needs. The Committee recently reviewed the 
results of the 2025 Point-In-Time (PIT) Count, noting a significant improvement in Cheyenne’s outreach: 
34 individuals were interviewed in 2025, compared to just 5 in 2024. Despite this progress, challenges 
remain in reaching certain populations, such as people residing in cars who may feel intimidated to 
participate, as well as those staying in hotels. The Committee is currently developing targeted strategies 
to better reach and accurately count these groups. 
 
The Community Development Manager (CDM) will serve on the 2026 PIT Count Planning Committee and 
act as Secretary, coordinating and attending monthly meetings from May 2025 through January 2026. 
The CDM will collaborate with members of the PIT Count Committee and the Wyoming Continuum of 
Care (CoC) to develop effective strategies for engaging hard-to-reach unsheltered individuals, 
connecting them with services, and participating directly in interviews during the January 2026 PIT 
Count. 
 
Additionally, the CDM will attend Wyoming CoC meetings and will participate in CoC committees, such 
as the Data Analysis Committee, to further assess and address the specific needs of unsheltered 
populations. Ongoing outreach efforts will include visiting food kitchens and conducting interviews with 
homeless individuals to gain deeper insight into their needs. The CDM will share this information with 
the service delivery network to help improve outcomes for those experiencing homelessness. 
Addressing the emergency and transitional housing needs of homeless persons 
The H&CD Division has a longstanding commitment to addressing the emergency shelter and 
transitional housing needs of homeless individuals and families. For Program Year 2025, the City 
allocated $60,000 in CDBG funds to support the Community Action of Laramie County’s Smith Manor 
Transitional Housing project, specifically to provide new, safe, and accessible sidewalks for the facility. 
Smith Manor offers 18 efficiency apartments for homeless individuals. Of these, 12 units serve as 
transitional housing for men and women working toward regaining stability and employment, with 
residents able to stay for up to two years. The remaining six apartments are designated for individuals 
with disabilities, who may reside there permanently. Rent is income-based: for residents with a monthly 
income of $400 or higher, rent is set at $240. For those earning less than $400 per month, rent is 

 
 
Annual Action Plan 
2025 
129 
OMB Control No: 2506-0117 (exp. 09/30/2021) 
calculated as a percentage of their income. The security deposit is $240, which can be paid in 
installments over four months to ease the financial burden on new tenants. 
 
Additionally, the City has allocated $12,483 in PY25 CDBG funds and $7,517 in matching funds—totaling 
$20,000—to support a local domestic violence shelter through utility reimbursement. The shelter 
anticipates serving approximately 60 clients each month. The grant covers utility costs for nine months, 
enabling the shelter to serve an estimated 540 clients annually. Clients include children, young adults, 
seniors, families, crime victims, homeless individuals, and people with special needs. The shelter has 
reported a 28% increase in bed nights occupied over the past two years, a trend attributed to economic 
challenges and a tightening housing market, which make it more difficult for victims to secure stable 
housing and achieve self-sufficiency. 
 
Through these targeted investments in transitional and emergency housing, the City continues to 
address critical housing needs and support vulnerable residents on their path to stability and 
independence. 
Helping homeless persons (especially chronically homeless individuals and 
families, families with children, veterans and their families, and unaccompanied 
youth) make the transition to permanent housing and independent living, 
including shortening the period of time that individuals and families experience 
homelessness, facilitating access for homeless individuals and families to 
affordable housing units, and preventing individuals and families who were 
recently homeless from becoming homeless again. 
The City is committed to helping homeless individuals transition to permanent housing and independent 
living through a collaborative, data-driven approach. By partnering with local organizations, the city will 
review recommendations from the 2025 Wyoming CoC Homeless Needs Assessment and analyze data 
specific to Cheyenne. This process will inform targeted strategies to reduce the duration of 
homelessness and prevent its recurrence among those recently housed. 
  
Additionally, the City will coordinate with other HUD-funded organizations, including the Cheyenne 
Housing Authority, the Wyoming Department of Health’s Communicable Disease Unit (which manages 
the Housing Opportunities for Persons With AIDS [HOPWA] program, a federal initiative that provides 
housing and related services for low-income individuals living with HIV/AIDS and their families), and 

 
 
Annual Action Plan 
2025 
130 
OMB Control No: 2506-0117 (exp. 09/30/2021) 
local recipients of CoC funding, to pool resources and expand access to affordable housing. These efforts 
are designed to create a coordinated response that addresses both the immediate and long-term needs 
of homeless individuals and families in the community. 
Help low-income individuals and families avoid becoming homeless, especially 
extremely low-income individuals and families who are likely to become homeless 
after being discharged from a publicly funded institution or system of care, or 
who are receiving assistance from public and private agencies that address 
housing, health, social services, employment, education or youth needs 
Cheyenne has some resources available to assist individuals discharged from publicly funded institutions 
and systems of care. Local nonprofits, such as Volunteers of America, work collaboratively to support 
these individuals as they transition back into the community. At the state level, the Wyoming Health 
Council partners with the Wyoming Department of Corrections to facilitate referrals to vital community 
resources and providers. These referrals have helped some individuals access essential services, 
including medication, primary care, housing, and employment opportunities. 
Currently, in Wyoming, no state program ensures individuals being released from an institution are 
connected to outpatient care and/or supportive services. However, Cheyenne Regional Medical Center 
is partnering with local public safety agencies to implement a comprehensive Medication-Assistance 
Treatment program. This initiative will provide continuous care for individuals with opioid use disorder 
from incarceration to community-based recovery services. The goal of this program is to reduce opioid 
related overdose deaths, improve recovery outcomes, and reduce recidivism. More consistency in 
discharge protocol planning and continuity of care is needed. Programs that offer wraparound services 
are critical to ensuring that gaps in benefits and services are reduced. 
The City is dedicated to helping low-income individuals and families, especially extremely low-income 
households and those exiting public institutions, avoid homelessness by: 
• 
Coordinating with Comea to support the establishment of transitional housing units for 
individuals leaving incarceration. 
• 
Utilize CDBG funds to promote digital learning and employment training programs for LMI 
individuals and families, including unaccompanied youth receiving assistance from agencies 
focused on housing, health, social services, employment, education, or youth needs. 
• 
Seek opportunities to coordinate with or provide CDBG assistance to programs to help them 
develop better continuity of care and wrap around services for individuals being discharged 
from a publicly funded institution or system of care, or who are receiving assistance from public 
and private agencies that address housing, health, social services, employment, education or 
youth needs 

 
 
Annual Action Plan 
2025 
131 
OMB Control No: 2506-0117 (exp. 09/30/2021) 
Through these collaborative initiatives, the City and its partners are committed to supporting vulnerable 
populations and preventing homelessness by ensuring equitable access to essential resources and 
services. 
 
 

 
 
Annual Action Plan 
2025 
132 
OMB Control No: 2506-0117 (exp. 09/30/2021) 
SP-65 Lead based paint Hazards – 91.215(i) 
Actions to address LBP hazards and increase access to housing without LBP 
hazards 
The City is actively working to reduce lead-based hazards and increase access to safe, lead-free housing 
through infrastructure upgrades, certified remediation, public education, and compliance with federal 
rules, 
Lead in Water: Identification and Replacement 
• 
The Cheyenne Board of Public Utilities (BOPU) is leading efforts to address the lead in the city’s 
water system. BOPU has mapped around 28,000 service line locations and created a Lead 
Service Line Inventory to identify which lines may contain lead. So far, only a small number of 
lead lines have been confirmed. 
• 
When a lead line is found or suspected, BOPU works directly with the property owner to replace 
it. Residents are notified if their property has a confirmed or suspected lead line. Those with 
confirmed lead lines receive educational materials and water filtration pitchers. 
• 
BOPU is also adjusting the water’s pH to minimize the risk of lead leaching from pipes into the 
water supply. The responsibility for service lines is shared: BOPU maintains the section from the 
water main to the curb stop, while homeowners are responsible for the section from the curb 
stop to their home. Regular water testing by BOPU shows that lead levels in Cheyenne’s water 
remain below action thresholds at most sites. 
Lead-Based Paint: Inspection and Remediation 
The Housing & Community Development (H&CD) Division requires lead paint inspections and 
remediation for rehabilitation projects in homes built before 1978. Certified inspectors and contractors, 
following EPA guidelines, handle these tasks. 
 
Homeowners receive information about the risks of lead-based paint, and local non-profits help with 
outreach and education. The City’s Economic Resource Administrator and Community Development 
Manager are seeking funding to help income-eligible property owners replace lead water lines. 
State and Community Support 
• 
The Wyoming Department of Health (WHD) runs a lead poisoning prevention program and 
recommends that all children in Wyoming be tested for lead at 12 and 24 months. The 
department provides resources on prevention, testing, and abatement. 

 
 
Annual Action Plan 
2025 
133 
OMB Control No: 2506-0117 (exp. 09/30/2021) 
• 
The Cheyenne Housing Authority (CHA) has already abated all lead-based paint in its public 
housing units by following the required regulations. 
How are the actions listed above related to the extent of lead poisoning and 
hazards? 
While it is very difficult to determine the exact extent of lead poisoning and hazards in the jurisdiction, 
the actions listed above demonstrate that BOPU, the City's H&CD Division, CHA and WDH are all working 
to reduce the risk of exposure from lead hazards in water, soil, and homes. These efforts include 
notifying residents about potential lead service lines, adjusting water chemistry to minimize lead 
leaching, and collaborating with property owners to identify and replace lead pipes. In addition, the 
WDH is working to improve blood lead testing, reporting, and surveillance, which will help ensure that 
cases of lead exposure are identified and managed more effectively. 
 
Lead is most commonly found in homes built before 1978. Nationally, around 44% of the U.S. housing 
stock was built before 1970, and in Cheyenne, about 35% of homes were built before 1970. This 
demonstrates that a significant number of homes in Cheyenne may contain lead, potentially exposing 
residents to lead hazards. While the extent of lead poisoning and hazards in Cheyenne is difficult to 
determine, the actions being taken by local agencies help to reduce these risks for Cheyenne residents. 
How are the actions listed above integrated into housing policies and procedures? 
BOPU’s measures focus on ensuring water safety, while their policies for resident notification and 
resource provision illustrate how these actions are embedded in operational protocols. H&CD Division’s 
policies require that all HUD-funded projects comply with federal standards for testing and remediation. 
Additionally, subrecipients are mandated to provide EPA Renovate Right information and actively 
educate clients and beneficiaries about the dangers of lead exposure and the importance of 
remediation. 
 

 
 
Annual Action Plan 
2025 
134 
OMB Control No: 2506-0117 (exp. 09/30/2021) 
SP-70 Anti-Poverty Strategy – 91.215(j) 
Jurisdiction Goals, Programs and Policies for reducing the number of Poverty-
Level Families 
Cheyenne does not have a formal anti-poverty strategy, but it implements a wide range of goals, 
programs, and policies across multiple departments to reduce the number of poverty-level families. 
These efforts have become especially important considering recent economic challenges, such as 
increased inflation and a rise in unemployment. While the City’s overall poverty rate is relatively low at 
about 9.3%, certain groups—including children, females aged 18-24, and persons with disabilities, face 
much higher rates of poverty. 
 
The City prioritizes support for children, women, people with disabilities, and seniors. Reducing 
childhood poverty is a central goal, with investments in programs such as the City’s youth programs, 
United Way, CDBG funding, and the Youth Alternatives Program. For women, especially those affected 
by domestic violence or single mothers, CDBG funds are used to support shelters, job training programs 
like Climb Wyoming, and victim assistance services. The Mayor’s Council for People with Disabilities 
focuses on improving accessibility and advocating for the needs of disabled residents, addressing 
barriers in employment, transportation, and public facilities. 
 
During the 2025-2027 Con Plan Period, the H&CD Division will use CDBG funds to support public services 
for LMI residents. These services include healthcare access, digital literacy, job training, domestic 
violence support, and senior meals. Infrastructure improvements, such as the Safe Routes to School 
Initiative and ADA upgrades to transitional housing, are also targeted to reduce poverty for LMI 
residents. 
 
The Youth Alternatives Department offers counseling, crisis intervention, mentoring, and academic 
support for at-risk youth and their families. Programs like the Foster Grandparent Program and the 
Academic Support and Enrichment Center provide mentoring, tutoring, and after-school activities to 
improve educational and emotional outcomes, reduce juvenile offenses, and strengthen family stability.  
For seniors, the City provides transportation and health services through partnerships with the 
Cheyenne Housing Authority and local transit programs, as well as providing annual funding for the 
Senior Activity Center. Employment initiatives, including partnerships with organizations like Dad’s 

 
 
Annual Action Plan 
2025 
135 
OMB Control No: 2506-0117 (exp. 09/30/2021) 
Making a Difference and the Laramie County Community College trucking program, help create job 
opportunities for residents facing employment barriers. 
 
These coordinated efforts are designed to address immediate needs, improve infrastructure, and 
support long-term self-sufficiency for families and individuals most at risk of poverty. 
How are the Jurisdiction poverty reducing goals, programs, and policies 
coordinated with this affordable housing plan 
Cheyenne’s poverty-reducing programs and policies are closely coordinated with its affordable housing 
plan. The City recognizes that LMI households are disproportionately cost-burdened and face significant 
barriers to securing affordable housing, with many spending more than 30% of their income on housing 
and having to make difficult choices between basic needs. 
 
The City’s CDBG-funded activities for Program Year 2025, including transitional housing facility upgrades, 
shelter support, job training for single mothers, and free digital literacy for LMI City residents, are 
intentionally designed to both prevent poverty and provide direct assistance to residents facing 
economic hardship. These activities are aligned with Con Plan goals, which explicitly support reducing 
poverty and fostering self-sufficiency. By reducing the number of barriers that LMI residents face, these 
efforts have a direct impact on access to affordable housing. 
 
 

 
 
Annual Action Plan

SP-80 Monitoring – 91.230 
Describe the standards and procedures that the jurisdiction will use to monitor 
activities carried out in furtherance of the plan and will use to ensure long-term 
compliance with requirements of the programs involved, including minority 
business outreach and the comprehensive planning requirements 
The City follows procedures established by HUD to ensure that all applicable laws, regulations, and 
eligibility requirements are met, including minority business outreach and comprehensive planning 
requirements. All CDBG funds are managed by the H&CD Division and disbursed by the City’s Finance 
Department. Expenses are drawn down monthly using the Integrated Disbursement and Information 
System (IDIS), which also serves as the platform for reporting program performance. This system 
ensures that all financial transactions are transparent, well-documented, and compliant with federal 
guidelines. The H&CD Division maintains systems for tracking and data collection to support its 
comprehensive planning efforts. All activities are reviewed to ensure alignment with HUD’s planning 
requirements and the City’s own strategic priorities. 
 
The H&CD Division ensures ongoing compliance with HUD program requirements and is responsible for 
tracking the progress of all funded activities. Monitoring is conducted in several forms, including desk 
reviews, phone and email communications, and on-site visits. These procedures are detailed in the City’s 
Sub-Grantee Procedures manual. For each new General Service Project, the Community Development 
Manager (CDM) conducts at least two on-site visits. The first visit, held before the project begins, 
focuses on reviewing the subrecipient’s accounting system, understanding financial requirements, 
required policies and procedures, and record-keeping practices. Later visits are to evaluate the project’s 
progress, take site photos, ensure compliance by conducting interviews with laborers and meet with 
agency and construction staff, as needed. 
 
Ongoing projects and experienced subrecipients are monitored at least once per program year through 
on-site visits that focus on financial and programmatic compliance. Subrecipients are also required to 
submit regular performance status reports, which provide updates on progress, identify any issues or 
delays, and include demographic information on clients served, such as race, ethnicity, and income. 
 
 

 
 
Annual Action Plan 
2025 
137 
OMB Control No: 2506-0117 (exp. 09/30/2021) 
 
AP-15 Expected Resources – 91.220(c)(1,2) 
Introduction 
An annual action plan, included within a Consolidated Plan (Con Plan) for a Community Development 
Block Grant (CDBG) entitlement, is a required document that outlines how a jurisdiction will use its 
allocation of federal CDBG funds during a single program year to address local housing and community 
development priorities. The AP-15 Section of the Action Plan for the City of Cheyenne’s 2025-2027 Con 
Plan details how the City will allocate HUD CDBG funds during the first year of the Con Plan, the 2025 
Program Year. 
During the 2025 Program Year (PY25), the City will allocate CDBG funds to the following eligible 
activities: 
• 
Neighborhood and infrastructure improvements 
• 
Public facility enhancements 
• 
Public services 
• 
Job training and economic development 
• 
Administration and planning 
These CDBG activities were recommended by the Housing & Community Development (H&CD) Division’s 
Advisory Council to the City Council, as they meet HUD requirements for eligible activities and fulfill a 
National Objective to benefit low- to moderate-income individuals or households. Additionally, these 
activities are identified as goals and priority needs in the Con Plan. 
The allocation process is conducted through an annual grant cycle and includes opportunities for citizen 
participation and public comment. The allocation of resources is broken out in the Anticipated 
Resources Table and the narrative sections below.

• 
The “Program Income” column shows $0.00 because the City does not receive program income. 
• 
The “Annual Allocation” of $416,012, reflects the actual amount the City received from HUD for 
the 2025 Program Year. The City received the official allocation letter from the HUD CPD Region 
8 Office in Denver via email on Monday, May 19, 2025. 
• 
The $298,580 listed in the “Prior Year Resources” column consists of $39,527.20 in unused CDBG 
funds, which the City will allocate to the Safe Routes to School project and $259,000 for a 
Supplemental Grant Cycle. 
• 
The amount shown in the “Expected Amount Available in the Con Plan” column, $832,024, is 
calculated by taking the actual allocation for Program Year 2025 ($416,112) and using that

and local funds), including a description of how matching requirements will be

The City will maximize federal resources by combining HUD allocations with local matches and 
collaborative investments to increase the impact of limited CDBG funds. Here's how funds will be 
leveraged: 
Public Service Funding Match: 
•

• 
Future Funding Match: If HUD allocations remain stable in 2026–2027, the City will continue 
matching funds to sustain these critical services for vulnerable populations. 
CDBG Program Administration:

139

• 
Compliance Focus: $83,222 allocated to administration, meeting HUD’s 20% cap, ensures 
efficient program delivery. 
• 
Cost Sharing: The City supplements this by covering part of a Community Development 
Manager’s salary/benefits through its General Fund. 
Safe Routes to School Infrastructure Project: 
• 
$250,000 CDBG Investment: provides CDBG funds for critical safety improvements for the Alta 
Vista Elementary School in a low-to-moderate income (LMI) neighborhood, including $39,527 in 
repurposed prior-year funds. The City will cover the costs of staff time and project management 
for the Traffic Engineer and a portion of the salaries of the H&CD staff. 
• 
FORMAK Foundation: Secures additional grants to update Wyoming’s Safe Routes plan and fund 
implementation projects. 
• 
The City of Cheyenne’s “5th Penny” tax is an optional 1% sales and use tax approved by voters 
to be used for general purposes, specifically for road maintenance and improvements.  
Supplemental Grant Opportunities 
• 
$250,000 Shovel-Ready Projects: Unspent CDBG funds will finance infrastructure/public facilities 
serving LMI communities. 
• 
Priority Criteria: Projects which demonstrate multi-resource leverage (e.g., state grants, private 
donations) and serve vulnerable groups will be prioritized for funding. 
This integrated strategy ensures every federal dollar attracts complementary investments, driving 
greater equity and scalability in community development efforts.

The Safe Routes to School Project will be implemented within City rights-of-way, utilizing publicly owned 
land to support infrastructure and facility improvements. These enhancements will directly benefit LMI 
neighborhoods in Cheyenne, Wyoming. This project addresses a priority need to improve low-income 
neighborhoods and aligns with the goal of enhancing infrastructure, as identified in the Con Plan.

Introduction  
The AP-35 Projects Section of the PY25 Action Plan provides details about the projects selected to 
receive CDBG funding for the City’s upcoming program year, and the amount awarded to each project. 
Additionally, the annual goals and priority-needs are identified and the anticipated number of 
beneficiaries that the activity will benefit. Additionally, reasons for allocation priorities and obstacles in 
addressing the City’s underserved needs are discussed. 
Projects

Project Name

2 Safe Routes to School-Alta Vista Elementary School Safety Improvements 
3 Meals On Wheels: More than a Meal for Laramie County Seniors

Table 52 - Project Information 
 
Describe the reasons for allocation priorities and any obstacles to addressing 
underserved needs 
The City’s action plan priorities are shaped by input from nonprofits, community organizations, survey 
data, and City staff. The primary challenge in addressing underserved needs is limited funding, which is 
further strained by rising costs and inflation impacting Cheyenne’s low- and moderate-income (LMI) 
residents. Additionally, many nonprofits find CDBG regulations complex, which discourages some from 
pursuing larger, more impactful projects. 
 
To help overcome these barriers, the H&CD Division offers technical support and outreach, assisting 
organizations in navigating requirements and encouraging more applications. Nonprofits that receive 
funding are enthusiastic about meeting community needs and value the ongoing impact of CDBG 
funding in Cheyenne. 
 
Allocation priorities were determined through debriefs on the Con Plan Consultations between H&CD 

 
 
Annual Action Plan 
2025 
143 
OMB Control No: 2506-0117 (exp. 09/30/2021) 
staff and the H&CD Advisory Council. For the Program Year 2025 (PY25) grant cycle, the H&CD Division 
accepted nineteen Letters of Intent (LOI). The Council reviewed these and determined that all proposed 
activities met HUD eligibility requirements and the National Objective to serve LMI residents. As a result, 
all agencies submitting LOIs were invited to submit full applications. However, no agencies applied for 
acquisition or rehabilitation for housing, likely due to capacity limitations and concerns about regulatory 
requirements for larger projects (such as BAP, environmental review, Davis-Bacon, and Section 3 
compliance). 
 
Based on these consultations, the H&CD staff and Advisory Council prioritized activities that not only 
address identified needs but also have willing and capable organizations ready to meet HUD 
requirements. Additionally, their goal was to shorten the Con Plan cycle from five to three years and use 
the next three years to educate and engage housing developers about CDBG opportunities, laying the 
groundwork for future partnerships and grant applications. 
 
When the PY25 grant applications were received in January, the Advisory Council reviewed 15 
applications, including a large project submitted by the City. There was a significant increase in eligible 
Public Service applications, and with support from the City Economic Coordinator and the Mayor, 
matching funds from the H&CD Reserve funds were approved so that five public service projects could 
each be awarded $20,000. This commitment ensures that the City can better meet priority needs 
identified in the Con Plan.

AP-50 Geographic Distribution – 91.220(f)  
Description of the geographic areas of the entitlement (including areas of low-
income and minority concentration) where assistance will be directed  
Assistance will be directed to Qualified Census Tracts (QCTs) and Low- and Moderate-Income (LMI) block 
groups. Within the City of Cheyenne, targeting is based on analysis of local data included on the HUD 
CPD map tool, which identifies areas of need. 
 
For the 2025 Program Year, activities have been funded in the following areas: 
 Tract 7, Block Group 1 (56021000701): 
• 
Located just east of downtown Cheyenne, this tract is bounded by Pershing Boulevard to the 
north, 15th Street to the south, and is adjacent to the Union Pacific rail yard, which separates 
Cheyenne’s north and south sides. The tract’s eastern boundary runs from Evans Avenue on the 
west to Logan Avenue on the east. This predominantly residential area includes Holiday Park 
and the Cheyenne Family YMCA. CDBG funds will support critical school safety improvements at 
Alta Vista Elementary School, situated between Rollins and Logan Avenue, just east of Holiday 
Park and the YMCA. 
Tract 7 Block Group 2 (56021000702): 
• 
West of Tract 7.02, this area contains the offices of Community Action of Laramie County, which 
operates the Smith Manor Transitional Housing program. For Program Year 2025, CDBG funds 
are allocated for sidewalk replacement to improve resident safety. The City of Cheyenne 
Municipal Building (2101 O’Neil Ave.) is also located in this tract, which houses the Housing & 
Community Development Division. While Smith Manor itself is not in a QCT or LMI block, its 
residents, homeless individuals in transitional housing, qualify as a HUD presumed benefit 
category.

Rationale for the priorities for allocating investments geographically  
The City of Cheyenne is prioritizing Community Development Block Grant (CDBG) funding in low- and 
moderate-income (LMI) block groups and census tracts. This approach ensures that CDBG funds are 
directed toward area benefit projects, particularly for larger infrastructure and public facility 
improvements, in compliance with program requirements. The city is dedicated to investing these 
resources in primarily residential LMI neighborhoods, so that LMI families directly experience the 
benefits of these investments.

AP-55 Affordable Housing – 91.220(g)  
Introduction 
The City has allocated funds for the Smith Manor Transitional Housing Sidewalk Repair and Replacement 
project, which will upgrade transitional housing facilities and benefit approximately 18 individuals each 
year. 
During the previous Con Planning period, CDBG funds were allocated for home rehabilitation. 
Rehabilitation is not included as a priority need in the 2025-2027 Con Plan as the subrecipient 
experienced challenges coordinating the repairs projects, particularly, issues meeting extensive 
environmental review requirements. The funds were recently returned and will be used to fund facility 
rehabilitation or infrastructure improvements that benefit low- to moderate- income households. 
Other affordable housing activities, such as the acquisition of existing units, were also excluded from the 
upcoming plan due to difficulties in spending previously allocated funds, largely because of complex 
environmental review requirements and limited affordable housing inventory. The City is reassessing 
which activities can be implemented more efficiently and is seeking ways to better align projects with 
regulatory requirements and local organizational capacity. Additionally, the City will begin working with 
consulting agencies to conduct CEST-level and EA-level environmental reviews for larger projects. This 
approach will relieve staff of this responsibility and allow experts with specialized knowledge of 
environmental regulations to complete the work. 
 
Rental assistance remains an important priority. While applications for the 2025 program year were 
submitted by nonprofits to provide rental assistance, the H&CD Advisory Council prioritized other public 
service needs due to high demand, organizational capacity, and past performance considerations. The 
City remains committed to partnering with strong organizations and encourages future project 
proposals that provide public facility or infrastructure improvements for LMI households, ensuring CDBG 
resources continue to support Cheyenne’s affordable housing and community development goals.

AP-60 Public Housing – 91.220(h) 
Introduction 
 The Cheyenne Housing Authority (CHA) plays a crucial role in addressing the need for low-income 
housing in Cheyenne. CHA’s mission is to provide decent, safe, and sanitary housing to elderly, disabled, 
and economically disadvantaged families who cannot secure housing through conventional means. CHA 
manages 266 public housing units in Cheyenne, including 97 scattered-site single-family homes and 
multifamily properties for seniors and disabled tenants. CHA also owns and operates three WRAP 
houses for larger households and provides market-rate rental units, including 32 townhomes for the 
elderly in Foxcrest II, seven single-family homes in Cheyenne, and 12 Weaver Road Apartments. The 
Housing Choice Voucher Program, administered by CHA, is the primary federal program assisting very 
low-income households, elderly, and disabled persons in Cheyenne to afford decent, safe, and sanitary 
housing in the private market. Subsidies are paid directly to landlords on behalf of eligible families, who 
pay the remaining rent difference. CHA also sponsors the Welcome Home Wyoming program, which has 
provided down payment assistance to more than 625 new homeowners. Additionally, CHA sponsored 
the formation of the Wyoming Housing Partnership, a nonprofit organization with ownership interest in 
88 units in Cheyenne, which CHA manages. 
Actions planned during the next year to address the needs to public housing 
During PY25, the City will not undertake any direct actions to address public housing needs. CHA, 
operating independently, is solely responsible for implementing the activities outlined in its own Annual 
and 5-Year Plans. However, the City has previously supported CHA, including providing CDBG assistance 
for HVAC upgrades during the 2020–2024 Con Plan period. While CHA did not apply for funding in the 
most recent grant cycle, the City remains open to assisting CHA with eligible projects in the future. 
The Community Development Manager maintains regular communication with the CHA Director to stay 
informed about public housing needs and priorities. If specific needs are identified during the 2025–
2027 Consolidated Plan period, and if funds are available, the City would consider amending the 
Consolidated Plan to support an eligible CHA project, provided a formal application is submitted and 
approved. The City’s role is thus limited to supporting broader community development and responding 
to public housing needs as opportunities and resources allow, rather than directly managing or funding 
public housing activities. 
Actions to encourage public housing residents to become more involved in 
management and participate in homeownership 
CHA has implemented several initiatives to increase resident involvement in management and support 
pathways to homeownership for public housing residents. These initiatives focus on providing 

 
 
Annual Action Plan 
2025 
156 
OMB Control No: 2506-0117 (exp. 09/30/2021) 
opportunities for residents to participate in decision-making and develop skills for economic self-
sufficiency. 
One initiative is the Resident Advisory Council (RAC), which CHA encourages at each of its multifamily 
rental properties. The RAC serves as a forum for residents to provide feedback and discuss issues related 
to the operation of the public housing program. Through regular meetings, residents can raise concerns, 
suggest improvements, and participate in discussions about their communities. 
CHA also offers the Family Self-Sufficiency (FSS) Program to participants in both the Housing Choice 
Voucher and Public Housing programs. The FSS Program provides resources and support for residents to 
work toward financial independence. This includes assistance with employment skills, household 
budgeting, and other tools to help participants achieve economic self-sufficiency and potentially move 
toward homeownership. 
In addition, CHA provides ongoing training for both staff and residents. Staff members receive regular 
Fair Housing training, while tenants have access to workshops on topics such as housekeeping, being a 
good neighbor, and energy conservation. These trainings are intended to help residents maintain 
successful tenancies and contribute positively to their communities. 
Alongside the efforts of CHA, the City of Cheyenne is actively working to involve residents and support 
community development. The Housing & Community Development Division recently surveyed seniors at 
the Senior Center to gather input on priority needs for the upcoming Consolidated Plan, ensuring that 
resident perspectives are included in local planning. The City also supports organizations like Habitat for 
Humanity and My Front Door, which offer programs such as financial literacy classes, homeownership 
counseling, and financial planning workshops. The Blue Boost Center is a wraparound service resource 
center who also provides financial literacy counseling. These programs are designed to help residents 
build financial stability and achieve homeownership. 
Furthermore, the City has invested in an updated Analysis of Impediments to Fair Housing Study in 
collaboration with the University of Wyoming Center for Business & Economic Analysis. To share the 
findings, a public “lunch and learn” event was held, with plans to further disseminate the information 
and work with community partners on actionable steps in the coming year. Recommendations from the 
study will be incorporated into future annual action plans as the City continues to promote fair housing, 
financial empowerment, and inclusive community development for all residents. 
If the PHA is designated as troubled, describe the manner in which financial 
assistance will be provided or other assistance  
The Cheyenne Housing Authority (CHA) is currently designated as a Standard Public Housing Authority 
(PHA) and is not classified as "troubled." As a result, Community Development Block Grant (CDBG) 
financial assistance is not being allocated to CHA for the upcoming year. 
 
 

 
 
Annual Action Plan 
2025 
157 
OMB Control No: 2506-0117 (exp. 09/30/2021) 
 
AP-65 Homeless and Other Special Needs Activities – 91.220(i) 
Introduction 
The City of Cheyenne’s annual action plan for Program Year 2025 outlines a comprehensive, multi-
faceted strategy to reduce and ultimately end homelessness in the community. Through targeted 
investments in outreach, emergency shelter, transitional housing, and supportive services, the plan 
addresses both immediate needs and the root causes of housing instability. Funding allocations from the 
Housing & Community Development Advisory Council and Community Development Block Grant (CDBG) 
support a range of initiatives, including job training for single mothers, digital literacy education, 
language interpretation for healthcare access, and meal delivery for vulnerable seniors. These efforts 
are complemented by infrastructure improvements and operational support for shelters serving 
individuals and families at risk of or experiencing homelessness. 
 
By focusing on high-risk populations, such as single mothers, seniors, individuals with disabilities, and 
survivors of domestic violence, the City aims to prevent homelessness before it occurs and shorten the 
duration for those already unhoused. Despite challenges posed by limited federal resources, Cheyenne’s 
approach prioritizes the removal of barriers to stability, ensuring that the most vulnerable residents 
receive the support needed to achieve long-term housing security and independence. 
Describe the jurisdictions one-year goals and actions for reducing and ending 
homelessness including reaching out to homeless persons (especially unsheltered 
persons) and assessing their individual needs 
The Housing & Community Development Advisory Council has allocated funding to initiatives that will 
reduce or prevent homelessness. These initiatives address the need for job training, digital literacy 
education, language interruptive services for access to healthcare and meals for vulnerable populations. 
 
Climb Wyoming’s Cheyenne program received 2025 funding to provide free job training and counseling 
to low-income single mothers. This initiative addresses the root causes of homelessness for this 
vulnerable group by empowering participants to secure stable, higher-wage employment and achieve 
self-sufficiency. Outcomes include a 91% full-time employment rate for graduates, with most 
participants doubling their wages within two years. At least 85% of enrollees are expected to 
successfully complete the program, and graduates report significant reductions in dependence on 
government benefits, including subsidized childcare, Medicaid, and SNAP. By targeting single mothers—

 
 
Annual Action Plan 
2025 
158 
OMB Control No: 2506-0117 (exp. 09/30/2021) 
who head 20% of Laramie County households with children, 12% of whom live below the poverty line—
the program proactively prevents homelessness for families at high risk. 
 
CDBG funds support language interpretation services at HealthWorks, a federally qualified health center 
serving homeless individuals, people with disabilities, and those with special needs, regardless of their 
ability to pay. In 2024, 32% of HealthWorks’ patients fell at or below 200% of the Federal Poverty Line, 
including many uninsured individuals. Enhanced communication with case managers helps address 
social determinants of health—such as housing, employment, and access to resources—critical for 
stabilizing those experiencing or at risk of homelessness. 
 
Meals on Wheels was awarded funding to provide meals and outreach to about 300 seniors, most of 
whom live below the poverty line. The program specifically targets residents over 65 who receive SNAP 
benefits, helping their food assistance stretch further. Collaboration with hospitals, doctors, and other 
agencies ensures wrap-around services and holistic support for seniors at risk of homelessness. 
 
The Digital Literacy Program for Adult Education at Laramie County Community College offers free 
digital skills training to low-to-moderate income residents, including the homeless, non-English 
speakers, and people with special needs. By equipping approximately 150 city residents with essential 
technology skills, the program enhances employability and economic stability, reducing the risk of 
homelessness. 
Addressing the emergency shelter and transitional housing needs of homeless 
persons 
The City is allocating Program Year 2025 funds to two projects that directly address the emergency 
shelter and transitional housing needs of homeless individuals and families. 
 
Smith Manor Transitional Housing, operated by Community Action of Laramie County, offers 18 
efficiency apartments for people experiencing homelessness, with a focus on supporting low- to 
moderate-income young adults (ages 18–24), seniors (62+), and individuals with special needs. 
Residents benefit from transitional housing paired with intensive case management, helping them work 
toward permanent housing and increasing self-sufficiency. The program is designed to provide a stable 
environment and support services that foster independence and long-term stability. 

 
 
Annual Action Plan 
2025 
159 
OMB Control No: 2506-0117 (exp. 09/30/2021) 
The Smith Manor Sidewalk Restoration project, funded with Program Year 2025 Community 
Development Block Grant (CDBG) funds, will repair and replace sidewalks and walkways used by 
residents to access their apartments. This investment improves safety and accessibility for all residents, 
including those with mobility challenges, and will have a lasting impact by enhancing quality of life and 
supporting long-term stability as residents transition out of homelessness. 
 
Additionally, public service funds are allocated to a local domestic violence shelter to reimburse utility 
costs. This ensures the shelter remains operational for children, families, seniors, crime victims, and 
individuals with special needs, all of whom have incomes below 80% of the area median. The shelter 
serves approximately 60 clients per month, providing a safe environment and essential services for 
those who are in dangerous situations and facing immediate risk of homelessness. 
 
Together, these projects utilize CDBG funding to address the emergency shelter and transitional housing 
needs of homeless individuals and families, supporting pathways to stability and self-reliance. 
Helping homeless persons (especially chronically homeless individuals and 
families, families with children, veterans and their families, and unaccompanied 
youth) make the transition to permanent housing and independent living, 
including shortening the period of time that individuals and families experience 
homelessness, facilitating access for homeless individuals and families to 
affordable housing units, and preventing individuals and families who were 
recently homeless from becoming homeless again 
The Smith Manor Sidewalk project supports Community Action of Laramie County (CALC), a key 
Cheyenne nonprofit dedicated to reducing homelessness and preventing these individuals from 
becoming homeless again by helping them to achieve self-sufficiency. CALC provides stable transitional 
housing and supportive services to chronically homeless individuals, veterans, young adults, and 
families, helping them move into permanent housing and independent living. 
 
CALC’s success is built on strong community partnerships that deliver wraparound services tailored to 
each client’s needs. These collaborations connect clients with affordable housing and essential 
resources through organizations such as the Cheyenne Housing Authority, Department of Veterans 
Affairs, Volunteers of America Northern Rockies, Department of Family Services, local healthcare 

 
 
Annual Action Plan 
2025 
160 
OMB Control No: 2506-0117 (exp. 09/30/2021) 
providers, shelters, and various volunteer agencies. 
 
Through these partnerships, CALC links residents to vital services including case management, 
healthcare, employment assistance, mental health counseling, and financial guidance. The City’s 
allocation of Community Development Block Grant (CDBG) funds to Smith Manor strengthens CALC’s 
transitional housing program, providing residents with a safe environment and the comprehensive 
support needed to build life skills and achieve self-sufficiency. This assistance helps residents plan for 
long-term stability and reduces the risk of returning homelessness. 
 
CDBG funding for sidewalk repairs at Smith Manor also enhances the safety and accessibility of the 
property, ensuring residents can move about securely. These improvements help CALC maintain its 
financial commitments and continue delivering high-quality housing and support services to those in 
need. 
Helping low-income individuals and families avoid becoming homeless, especially 
extremely low-income individuals and families and those who are: being 
discharged from publicly funded institutions and systems of care (such as health 
care facilities, mental health facilities, foster care and other youth facilities, and 
corrections programs and institutions); or, receiving assistance from public or 
private agencies that address housing, health, social services, employment, 
education, or youth needs. 
The City’s Housing & Community Development Division works closely with local clinics and service 
providers to identify and address gaps in healthcare, shelter, and supportive services. The City has 
allocated CDBG funds to cover essential utility costs for a local domestic violence shelter. This CDBG 
investment ensures that the shelter remains operational and able to provide a haven for children, 
families, seniors, crime victims, and individuals with special needs, all of whom are living below 80% of 
the area median income. The shelter serves approximately 60 clients each month, offering protection 
and essential services to those fleeing unsafe environments and facing the immediate risk of 
homelessness. By providing this support to the shelter, the City is assisting the shelter in supporting 
long-term stability for survivors and their children. 
 
While limited federal funding sometimes restricts the ability to offer direct homelessness prevention 
services, and there are limited programs that directly serve extremely low-income individuals or those 

 
 
Annual Action Plan 
2025 
161 
OMB Control No: 2506-0117 (exp. 09/30/2021) 
being discharged from institutions, the City’s ongoing efforts, including shelter support, are designed to 
align with best practices for preventing homelessness among the most vulnerable. 
 
 
 

 
 
Annual Action Plan 
2025 
162 
OMB Control No: 2506-0117 (exp. 09/30/2021) 
AP-75 Barriers to affordable housing – 91.220(j) 
Introduction:  
After consulting with more than 30 community stakeholders, non-profits, and public agencies, the City 
of Cheyenne has developed thoughtful and achievable actions to address the negative impacts of public 
policies that create barriers to affordable housing. Building on recommendations from the Mayor’s 
Affordable Housing Task Force, the City has made significant progress in updating the Unified 
Development Code to better support housing development. The Mayor, City Council, department 
directors, and staff recognize the challenges posed by the current housing shortage for Cheyenne’s 
residents and businesses. They remain committed to collaborating with partners to accelerate housing 
development and expand the supply of affordable units in the community. 
Actions it planned to remove or ameliorate the negative effects of public policies 
that serve as barriers to affordable housing such as land use controls, tax policies 
affecting land, zoning ordinances, building codes, fees and charges, growth 
limitations, and policies affecting the return on residential investment 
Below are the actions the City of Cheyenne has planned for the 2025 Program Year to remove or reduce 
the negative effects of public policies that serve as barriers to affordable housing, such as land use 
controls, tax policies affecting land, zoning ordinances, building codes, fees and charges, growth 
limitations, and policies affecting the return on residential investment: 
• 
Identify funding opportunities and provide technical support to affordable housing developers. 
• 
Provide updates on Cheyenne’s barriers to affordable housing development to state legislators 
and advocate for policies and funding that support affordable housing and economic 
development. 
• 
Explore strategies to streamline local permitting and zoning processes. 
• 
Develop public engagement tools to educate the community about city projects and initiatives, 
and to provide clear, consistent information to developers. 
• 
Allocate CDBG funds to support infrastructure and facility upgrades for transitional housing, as 
well as housing for seniors and vulnerable populations. 
• 
Participate in long-term planning meetings for affordable housing projects involving multiple 
stakeholders, with a focus on developing housing for vulnerable populations such as seniors and 
persons with disabilities. 
• 
Explore innovative collaborations between public and private affordable housing developers and 
municipalities that result in impactful affordable housing development projects like Hope 
Springs in Greely, CO. 
 
 

 
 
Annual Action Plan 
2025 
163 
OMB Control No: 2506-0117 (exp. 09/30/2021) 
AP-85 Other Actions – 91.220(k) 
Introduction:  
Each year, the Housing & Community Development (H&CD) Division works in partnership with the H&CD 
Advisory Council to assess community needs and establish funding priorities. This collaborative process 
includes public participation and consultation with local agencies to ensure that funding decisions reflect 
Cheyenne’s current priority needs and goals. The CDBG application cycle typically begins in October with 
information sessions, followed by a single round of applications accepted in November. Applications are 
reviewed using a standardized scoring system, and funding recommendations are made in January. 
Funds are awarded to subrecipients and become available the following September. 
 
For Program Year 2025, the H&CD Division is prioritizing projects that provide essential public services 
and infrastructure and public facility improvements. All funded activities for the 2025 Program Year are 
designed to benefit low- and moderate-income individuals or households.  
 
These funded activities leverage limited CDBG Entitlement funds with local resources, maximizing the 
positive impact on Cheyenne residents’ quality of life. 
 
Any unused funds from a program year are carried forward and added to the next funding cycle, 
ensuring that all available resources are fully utilized to address the city’s most pressing community 
development needs. 
Actions planned to address obstacles to meeting underserved needs 
The City plans to overcome obstacles to meeting the needs of underserved populations through a 
comprehensive, collaborative approach. All 2025 activities funded by the CDBG programs are 
intentionally designed to serve low- and moderate-income households, seniors, youth, victims of 
domestic violence, people experiencing homelessness, and other vulnerable groups. To maximize 
impact, the City is providing additional funding to activities that receive public service awards. 
Recognizing the federal 15% cap on public services, the City will contribute around $35,000 annually 
during the 2025–2027 Consolidated Planning period, using designated H&CD Housing Program funds. 
This supplemental funding will enable more organizations to access critical resources and expand 

 
 
Annual Action Plan 
2025 
164 
OMB Control No: 2506-0117 (exp. 09/30/2021) 
services to those most in need. 
 
The H&CD Division offers ongoing technical assistance to local nonprofits and agencies interested in 
applying for CDBG funds. This support continues throughout the grant management process, ensuring 
compliance with federal requirements and helping partner organizations build capacity. By assisting 
organizations in navigating regulatory requirements and overcoming administrative challenges, the City 
facilitates greater access to funding and encourages broader program participation. 
 
Strong partnerships with city departments, local nonprofits, and service organizations are integral to 
identifying needs, coordinating services, and developing strategies to meet the needs of underserved 
populations. H&CD staff actively participate in local initiatives such as the Point-In-Time Count, the 
Laramie County Community Partnership Housing Task Force, and the WyoNAHRO Board to ensure 
alignment of efforts and effective resource leveraging. Ongoing communication and consultation with a 
broad range of stakeholders-including organizations serving individuals with disabilities (such as Magic 
City, HealthWorks, and CALC), those supporting people experiencing homelessness (including 
Safehouse, Comea, Family Promise, Needs, and the Unaccompanied Student Initiative), and additional 
partners such as Recover Wyoming, Habitat for Humanity, Climb Wyoming, and Laramie County 
Community College (LCCC)-ensure that program planning and implementation remain responsive to 
evolving community needs. 
 
These coordinated actions reflect the City’s ongoing commitment to evaluating performance, adjusting 
strategies, and leveraging partnerships to remove obstacles to meeting unserved needs and improve 
outcomes for Cheyenne’s underserved residents. 
Actions planned to foster and maintain affordable housing 
The City is prioritizing the improvement and rehabilitation of public facilities, including transitional 
housing, housing for special needs populations, and shelters, in response to the growing demand for 
services that address homelessness and housing instability. For Program Year 2025 (PY25), the City has 
allocated $60,000 to enhance sidewalks and accessibility at the Community Action of Laramie County’s 
(CALC) Smith Manor Apartments. These transitional housing units support extremely low-income 
residents who are transitioning out of homelessness. In addition to managing the housing development, 
CALC provides Smith Manor residents with comprehensive wrap-around services to help them achieve 
self-sufficiency. 
The City will also open a Supplemental or Mini Grant Cycle, offering up to $259,000 for shovel-ready 
infrastructure and public facility projects that benefit low- and moderate-income (LMI) housing and 

 
 
Annual Action Plan 
2025 
165 
OMB Control No: 2506-0117 (exp. 09/30/2021) 
serve vulnerable populations. This additional grant cycle is made possible by reallocating funds returned 
by Habitat for Humanity of Laramie County, along with unspent funding from previous program years. 
The City is committed to the timely expenditure of federal funds and is excited to offer this bonus grant 
round to support much-needed improvements to facilities and infrastructure that impact LMI residents. 
To further support these initiatives, the City has allocated CDBG funds to engage environmental review 
consultants with expertise in HUD CEST-level and Environmental Assessment (EA) reviews. These 
consultants will prepare HEROS reviews for each project, ensuring compliance with all regulatory 
requirements. By outsourcing these specialized reviews, the H&CD staff can dedicate more time to 
community engagement and inter-departmental collaboration, further strengthening the City’s 
institutional capacity. By providing this support, the City hopes to encourage nonprofits and housing 
developers, who may have been hesitant to apply for CDBG funds due to environmental requirements, 
to participate in future grant cycles for housing rehabilitation and acquisition activities. 
Actions planned to reduce lead-based paint hazards 
H&CD Division policies require all HUD-funded projects to comply with federal standards for testing and 
remediation. Additionally, subrecipients must provide EPA Renovate Right information and actively 
educate clients and beneficiaries about the dangers of lead exposure and the importance of 
remediation. The City’s Economic Resource Administrator and Community Development Manager plan 
to research potential federal funding for service line replacement, as well as other funding opportunities 
to establish a program that would replace lead lines for low- and moderate-income (LMI) homeowners. 
If funding sources are identified, the City will collaborate with the Cheyenne Board of Public Utility on 
this project. 
 
 

 
 
Annual Action Plan 
2025 
166 
OMB Control No: 2506-0117 (exp. 09/30/2021) 
 
Actions planned to reduce the number of poverty-level families 
The City’s plans to reduce the number of poverty-level families through providing employment training, 
digital literacy, support for survivors of domestic violence, and food security for seniors. 
Climb Wyoming Employment Training 
CDBG funds will continue to support Climb Wyoming, a statewide nonprofit that provides free, industry-
specific job training and career placement for low- and moderate-income single mothers. The program’s 
holistic approach includes not only technical and job skills training, but also mental health counseling 
and wraparound services such as childcare, transportation, and housing assistance. By equipping 
participants with marketable skills and supporting their transition into stable employment, Climb 
Wyoming directly addresses the root causes of poverty among single-parent families and helps break 
cycles of generational poverty. 
Laramie County Community College (LCCC) ACES Program 
CDBG funds have been allocated to provide free digital literacy and workforce development training. 
The Laramie County Community College Adult Career & Education Services (ACES) program offers free 
courses in basic literacy, high school equivalency preparation, English as a Second Language, and digital 
skills training. These programs are designed to help participants build foundational and advanced skills 
needed for higher-wage jobs in a digital economy, supporting long-term income growth and social 
mobility. 
Shelter Utility Reimbursement  
CDBG allocations will help cover essential operating costs for local domestic violence shelters. This 
shelter utility reimbursement support is critical for providing shelter that empowers survivors of 
domestic violence to achieve self-sufficiency. 
Meals on Wheels and Food Pantry Support 
The city directs CDBG funds to Meals on Wheels and local food pantries, ensuring that seniors and other 
low- and moderate-income residents have reliable access to nutritious food. These services help reduce 
the financial burden of grocery expenses, prevent malnutrition among seniors on fixed incomes, and 
support overall household stability. 
 
Collectively, these initiatives represent a coordinated effort to address both the immediate needs and 
the underlying barriers faced by families experiencing poverty, with a focus on sustainable employment, 

 
 
Annual Action Plan 
2025 
167 
OMB Control No: 2506-0117 (exp. 09/30/2021) 
digital inclusion, housing stability, and food security. 
Actions planned to develop institutional structure  
The City of Cheyenne is committed to strengthening its institutional framework to more effectively 
deliver federal funds and support Wyoming residents. The City collaborates closely with internal staff 
and departments to continuously refine its delivery systems for federal programs, ensuring compliance 
and maximizing community impact. 
Regional Collaboration and Best Practices 
• H&CD Division staff actively participate in the HUD Region 8 User Group, a network of professionals 
from jurisdictions receiving HUD funding. 
• This group facilitates the exchange of best practices and innovative strategies for managing federal 
grant funds efficiently. 
• The Community Development Manager (CDM) contributes to the planning of quarterly Region 8 User 
Group meetings, helping to select presentation topics that equip municipalities and jurisdictions with 
practical tools to navigate complex HUD regulations. 
Professional Development and Training 
• The CDM is committed to ongoing professional development and plans to pursue additional training in 
areas such as Davis-Bacon certified payroll review, Build America, Buy America (BABA), and Section 3 
compliance. 
• These trainings are designed to enhance the City’s capacity to meet federal requirements and ensure 
Actions planned to enhance coordination between public and private housing and social service 
agencies. 
 
During the 2025 Program Year, the H&CD Division will implement several initiatives to strengthen 
collaboration between public and private housing and social service agencies: 
WYNAHRO (Wyoming Chapter of NAHRO) 
• 
Board Participation: Serve on the Board of Directors to provide leadership and strategic 
direction. 
• 
Membership Drive & Statewide Meeting: Assist with the 2025 WyoNAHRO membership drive 
and help plan the statewide meeting. 
• 
Resource Development: Set up resource links on the WyoNAHRO website to facilitate 
information sharing. 
• 
Impact: These activities will enhance public and private housing efforts in Cheyenne and foster a 

 
 
Annual Action Plan 
2025 
168 
OMB Control No: 2506-0117 (exp. 09/30/2021) 
statewide network for sharing best practices and resources, ultimately benefiting Cheyenne’s 
low- and moderate-income (LMI) housing situation. 
Continuum of Care (CoC) 
• 
Active Engagement: Attend and participate in CoC meetings, trainings, and forums. 
• 
Data Committee: Serve on the CoC Data Committee to improve data-driven decision-making. 
• 
Impact: These efforts will enhance coordination among multiple service organizations in 
Cheyenne that support unhoused individuals and families. 
Point-in-Time (PIT) Count Committee 
• 
Committee Service: Serve on the PIT Count Committee and assist in coordinating the 2026 PIT 
Count. 
• 
Collaboration: Work closely with the City Compliance Department, Cheyenne Police 
Department, Laramie County, and various service organizations. 
• 
Impact: This collaboration will improve data collection and service coordination for unhoused 
clients in Cheyenne. 
Laramie County Community Partnership Housing Task Force 
• 
Task Force Participation: Attend regular meetings and participate in coordinating initiatives. 
• 
Agency Collaboration: Work with over twenty public and private housing and social service 
agencies. 
• 
Impact: These partnerships will advance affordable housing solutions in Cheyenne and Laramie 
County. 
Cheyenne Housing Authority (CHA) 
• 
Ongoing Communication: Maintain regular communication with CHA regarding activities and 
initiatives. 
• 
Focus Areas: Address the needs of low- to moderate-income individuals, special needs 
populations, and those experiencing homelessness.

Introduction:  
 The City of Cheyenne will not be receiving program income; therefore, there is no program income to 
report. It is estimated that 100% of CDBG funds for Program Year 2025, including those outlined in this 
Annual Action Plan, will be used for activities that benefit persons of low and moderate income. 
Regarding the Overall Benefit requirement, the City anticipates that activities for Program Years 2025–
2027 will benefit 100% low- and moderate-income (LMI) persons. This ensures that the required 
minimum overall benefit of 70% of CDBG funds used to benefit persons of low and moderate income 
will be met. The years covered by this Annual Action Plan are 2025, 2026, and 2027.
